It doesn't change the output at all, it's purely an API usage pattern, but the difference in pipeline startup time on large feature sets is dramatic enough that it caused SLA misses before I caught it.

GROUP: PySpark & Distributed Computing
Q: What is a join strategy in PySpark — sort-merge join vs broadcast join vs hash join?
A: Broadcast hash join: sends the smaller table to every executor, joins locally without any shuffle on the large side. Best when one side fits in executor memory — threshold 10 MB by default. Sort-merge join: both sides are sorted on the join key and merged — requires shuffling both sides. Spark's default for large-large joins. Works for any data size but expensive when both sides are large. Shuffle hash join: builds a hash table from the smaller side and probes it with the larger side after shuffling both — faster than sort-merge when one side is significantly smaller but doesn't need to broadcast. Spark automatically chooses based on estimated table sizes. At HPCL I mostly relied on the optimizer's choice and tuned by adjusting the broadcast threshold and ensuring table statistics were current — stale stats cause the optimizer to pick the wrong strategy.
GROUP: SQL, Oracle & Hive HQL
Q: What is a window function and how does it differ from GROUP BY?
A: The key difference is what happens to your rows. GROUP BY collapses — if I have 1.5 million cylinder fill records and group by plant, I get 55 rows. A window function keeps all 1.5 million rows intact and adds a computed column alongside each one. So I can see each individual fill record and its plant-level aggregate in the same result set. At HPCL this was exactly what I needed for deviation analysis — I had to compare each fill event against the plant's rolling average without losing the row-level detail. The OVER clause defines the window — which rows the function sees. Without window functions that kind of analysis requires a self-join, which is both slower and harder to read.

GROUP: SQL, Oracle & Hive HQL
Q: Explain ROW_NUMBER and give a real HPCL example.
A: ROW_NUMBER assigns a unique sequential integer to each row within a partition, ordered by whatever you specify. No ties — even identical values get different numbers. The classic use case I hit constantly at HPCL was getting the most recent record per entity — latest maintenance record per equipment tag, or most recent fill event per cylinder. The pattern: wrap a ROW_NUMBER() OVER (PARTITION BY equipment_id ORDER BY maintenance_date DESC) in a subquery, then filter WHERE rn = 1 in the outer query. This gives exactly one row per equipment_id without a correlated subquery. I also used it for deduplication — when SCADA retransmitted records, ROW_NUMBER let me keep only the first received copy and discard duplicates cleanly.

GROUP: SQL, Oracle & Hive HQL
Q: What is the difference between ROW_NUMBER, RANK, and DENSE_RANK with tied values?
A: Given distributor scores of 95, 88, 88, 72 ranked descending — ROW_NUMBER gives 1, 2, 3, 4 regardless of ties, always unique. RANK gives 1, 2, 2, 4 — tied rows share a rank but the next rank skips, reflecting the positional gap. DENSE_RANK gives 1, 2, 2, 3 — tied rows share a rank and the next rank doesn't skip. At HPCL when I built the distributor performance ranking for the 100-point scoring model I used DENSE_RANK — business users expected consecutive tier numbers. If 50 distributors tied at rank 2, I wanted rank 3 next, not rank 52. ROW_NUMBER I use for deduplication where I need strict uniqueness regardless of ties. RANK when skipping reflects meaningful positional gaps, like a sports leaderboard.

GROUP: SQL, Oracle & Hive HQL
Q: Explain LAG and LEAD with a real HPCL trend analysis example.
A: LAG accesses the value from a previous row within the partition, LEAD accesses a future row, both without self-joins. The syntax is LAG(column, offset, default) OVER (PARTITION BY col ORDER BY col). At HPCL I used LAG constantly for day-over-day demand trend analysis per plant: SELECT plant_id, fill_date, total_demand, LAG(total_demand, 1, 0) OVER (PARTITION BY plant_id ORDER BY fill_date) AS prev_day_demand, total_demand - LAG(total_demand, 1, 0) OVER (...) AS day_over_day_change FROM daily_plant_totals. The PARTITION BY plant_id is critical — without it, January 1st of Plant A would look back to December 31st of Plant B, which is meaningless. I used LEAD in maintenance analytics to compute the gap between consecutive service events per equipment.

GROUP: SQL, Oracle & Hive HQL
Q: What is NTILE and when would you use it for distributor tier analysis?
A: NTILE(n) divides rows within a partition into n roughly equal groups and assigns each a bucket number from 1 to n. I used it extensively at HPCL for distributor performance tiering — NTILE(4) OVER (ORDER BY annual_score DESC) placed the top 25% in bucket 1, the next 25% in bucket 2, and so on. That directly fed the gold, silver, bronze, and inactive tier classification for our 6,200+ distributors. I also used NTILE(10) for decile analysis — identifying the top 10% by revenue contribution for premium service programs. One nuance: if the rows don't divide evenly, the first buckets get one extra row, so bucket sizes can differ by one. For business reporting that's fine; just document it so stakeholders don't wonder why gold has 1,551 vs silver's 1,550.

GROUP: SQL, Oracle & Hive HQL
Q: What are FIRST_VALUE and LAST_VALUE — and what is the LAST_VALUE default frame trap?
A: FIRST_VALUE returns the value from the first row of the window frame, LAST_VALUE from the last row. The critical trap with LAST_VALUE is the default frame — it's ROWS BETWEEN UNBOUNDED PRECEDING AND CURRENT ROW, not the full partition. So for every row, LAST_VALUE returns that row's own value, not the partition's final value. Most people expect LAST_VALUE to give the most recent value in the partition, but you get the current row unless you explicitly add ROWS BETWEEN UNBOUNDED PRECEDING AND UNBOUNDED FOLLOWING. At HPCL I used FIRST_VALUE to find each equipment tag's first maintenance date and LAST_VALUE for the most recent, always including the explicit UNBOUNDED FOLLOWING frame. Without it the results look plausible but are completely wrong.

GROUP: SQL, Oracle & Hive HQL
Q: What is the OVER clause — what are PARTITION BY, ORDER BY, and the frame specification?
A: OVER defines the window a function operates on. PARTITION BY divides rows into independent groups — the function resets for each partition, just like GROUP BY but without collapsing rows. Without PARTITION BY, the entire result set is one window. ORDER BY defines ordering within each partition — required for rank functions and running totals. The frame specification — ROWS BETWEEN or RANGE BETWEEN — defines which rows relative to the current row are included in the calculation. ROWS BETWEEN UNBOUNDED PRECEDING AND CURRENT ROW gives a running total from the first row to the current one. ROWS BETWEEN 6 PRECEDING AND CURRENT ROW gives a 7-row moving window. At HPCL the 7-day moving average for demand forecasting used all three — partition by plant, order by date, rows between 6 preceding and current.

GROUP: SQL, Oracle & Hive HQL
Q: What is the difference between ROWS and RANGE in a window frame?
A: ROWS counts physical rows — ROWS BETWEEN 6 PRECEDING AND CURRENT ROW always includes exactly 7 rows regardless of their values. RANGE uses value distances on the ORDER BY column — RANGE BETWEEN INTERVAL '7' DAY PRECEDING AND CURRENT ROW includes all rows within the last 7 days of the current row's date, handling gaps in the data naturally. For time-series with gaps this distinction is significant. At HPCL our demand data sometimes had no fills on certain days — with ROWS, a 7-row window might span 9 calendar days because two days had no records. With RANGE and an interval specification, the window covers exactly the last 7 calendar days of available data. I default to RANGE with date intervals for any time-series window function in production SQL.

GROUP: SQL, Oracle & Hive HQL
Q: How do you compute a running total using window functions?
A: SUM(fill_volume) OVER (PARTITION BY plant_id ORDER BY fill_date ROWS BETWEEN UNBOUNDED PRECEDING AND CURRENT ROW). The UNBOUNDED PRECEDING means from the first row of the partition, CURRENT ROW means up to and including the current row — so the sum accumulates as you move down the date sequence. PARTITION BY plant_id resets the running total for each plant. At HPCL I used running totals for cumulative year-to-date cylinder production tracking — each daily record showed the total from January 1st up to that day alongside the individual day's volume. If you omit the ORDER BY in the window spec, SUM OVER just gives the partition total in every row — which is useful for computing percentage-of-total but is not a running total.

GROUP: SQL, Oracle & Hive HQL
Q: How do you compute a 7-day moving average using window functions?
A: AVG(fill_volume) OVER (PARTITION BY plant_id ORDER BY fill_date ROWS BETWEEN 6 PRECEDING AND CURRENT ROW). That includes the current row and the 6 preceding — a 7-row moving window. For the first 6 rows of each partition the window is smaller since there aren't 6 preceding rows yet; Pandas returns NaN in this situation and SQL returns an average of whatever rows are available. If you want to enforce a minimum of 7 rows before computing, you'd wrap it in a CASE WHEN or use a HAVING clause in a subquery. At HPCL the 7-day and 30-day moving averages were core features in the demand forecasting model — smoothing out daily noise to expose the underlying trend the Prophet model was trying to capture.

GROUP: SQL, Oracle & Hive HQL
Q: Can you use a window function in a WHERE clause — and if not, how do you filter on a windowed result?
A: You can't use a window function directly in a WHERE clause — WHERE is evaluated before window functions in SQL's logical processing order, so the windowed values don't exist yet when WHERE runs. The standard solution is a subquery or CTE: compute the window function in an inner query, then filter on the result in the outer query. At HPCL I used this pattern constantly for deduplication — inner query computes ROW_NUMBER() OVER (PARTITION BY equipment_id ORDER BY received_timestamp DESC) AS rn, outer query filters WHERE rn = 1. Trying to put the ROW_NUMBER directly in the WHERE clause gives an ORA-30483 or equivalent error. Same rule applies in Hive HQL — window functions must be in SELECT, then filtered in an outer query.

GROUP: SQL, Oracle & Hive HQL
Q: What is PERCENT_RANK and CUME_DIST and when would you use them?
A: PERCENT_RANK gives the relative rank of each row as a value between 0 and 1 — (rank - 1) / (total rows - 1). The first row is always 0, the last is always 1. CUME_DIST gives the cumulative distribution — the fraction of rows with values less than or equal to the current row's value. PERCENT_RANK is useful when you want to say a distributor is in the 87th percentile of performance across the network. CUME_DIST is useful for identifying what percentage of distributors fall below a specific score threshold. At HPCL I used PERCENT_RANK when building the percentile-based distributor performance reports — it let regional managers immediately see where each distributor stood relative to the full network without any additional calculation.

GROUP: SQL, Oracle & Hive HQL
Q: What is a CTE and how do you write one?
A: A CTE — Common Table Expression — is a named subquery defined at the top of a statement using WITH. WITH procurement_base AS (SELECT vendor_id, item_code, po_price, pir_price FROM purchase_orders JOIN info_records USING (vendor_id, item_code)), variance_calc AS (SELECT *, po_price - pir_price AS price_variance FROM procurement_base) SELECT * FROM variance_calc WHERE price_variance > 0. Each CTE is defined once and referenced by name — you can chain multiple CTEs and each can reference the previous ones. At HPCL the procurement variance analysis had four logical steps — staging the raw joins, computing variance, classifying by category, and ranking — and CTEs let me write each step cleanly without nesting four levels of subqueries.

GROUP: SQL, Oracle & Hive HQL
Q: What is the difference between a CTE and a subquery — performance and readability?
A: A subquery is written inline inside another query — it can get deeply nested and becomes hard to read once you have more than two levels. A CTE is named and defined separately at the top, making the query read top-to-bottom like a series of logical steps. Readability is the main advantage. On performance: in most databases including Oracle, the optimizer can treat CTEs and subqueries identically — it may or may not materialize the CTE depending on the execution plan. In Oracle specifically a CTE is not always materialized; the optimizer can inline it if beneficial. In Hive, CTEs are generally inlined. For very complex multi-step analytical queries I always reach for CTEs because the code is maintainable — a subquery four levels deep is a maintenance hazard regardless of performance.

GROUP: SQL, Oracle & Hive HQL
Q: What is a recursive CTE and give a real hierarchical example?
A: A recursive CTE references itself to traverse hierarchical or graph-structured data. It has two parts joined by UNION ALL: an anchor member that seeds the recursion, and a recursive member that joins back to the CTE itself. At HPCL the organizational hierarchy — HQ, zone, region, plant — was a natural fit. WITH plant_hierarchy AS (SELECT plant_id, parent_id, plant_name, 0 AS level FROM org WHERE parent_id IS NULL UNION ALL SELECT c.plant_id, c.parent_id, c.plant_name, h.level + 1 FROM org c JOIN plant_hierarchy h ON c.parent_id = h.plant_id) SELECT * FROM plant_hierarchy. This traverses the full tree regardless of depth. I used a similar pattern to compute cumulative demand rolled up through the hierarchy — aggregating plant-level forecasts into regional and zone totals recursively.

GROUP: SQL, Oracle & Hive HQL
Q: When would you use a correlated subquery and what is its performance risk?
A: A correlated subquery references a column from the outer query, so it re-executes once per row of the outer query — making it O(n) in the number of outer rows. That's fine for small outer result sets but catastrophic on large tables. The classic use case is "get the most recent record per group" — which I always replace with ROW_NUMBER instead. Legitimate uses where I'd still consider a correlated subquery: EXISTS checks where I just need to know whether a match exists, not the matched values, because EXISTS short-circuits on the first match. At HPCL I rewrote several legacy correlated subqueries in the procurement analytics — replacing "WHERE EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM orders o WHERE o.vendor_id = v.vendor_id)" with proper joins, which cut query runtime from 45 minutes to under 3.

GROUP: SQL, Oracle & Hive HQL
Q: What is the difference between EXISTS and IN for subqueries?
A: IN evaluates the subquery fully and returns a list of values, then checks each outer row against that list. EXISTS checks for the presence of any matching row and short-circuits as soon as it finds one — it never retrieves the full result set. For large subqueries EXISTS is typically faster because it stops searching once a match is found. The semantic difference matters too: IN with a subquery that returns NULLs can produce unexpected results because a NOT IN with any NULL in the list returns no rows. NOT EXISTS doesn't have this problem. At HPCL I standardized on EXISTS for membership checks on large vendor tables and rewrote NOT IN conditions to NOT EXISTS specifically to avoid the NULL edge case, which had caused a compliance report to silently return zero rows for two months before I caught it.

GROUP: SQL, Oracle & Hive HQL
Q: How do you use a CTE for a multi-step analytical calculation?
A: I structure CTEs as a pipeline where each named block does one logical step and passes results to the next. For the HPCL procurement savings analysis: first CTE extracted raw PO and PIR data with joins, second CTE computed price variance per line item, third CTE classified each line item by spare category and flagged variance above threshold, fourth CTE aggregated savings by category. The final SELECT pulled from the fourth CTE with the summary I needed. This approach means I can debug any step by commenting out later CTEs and running to that point — each CTE is independently readable and testable. It also means a reviewer can follow the logic top-to-bottom without jumping between nested parentheses. For anything more than two logical steps I reach for CTEs automatically.

GROUP: SQL, Oracle & Hive HQL
Q: Explain all SQL join types with examples — INNER, LEFT, RIGHT, FULL OUTER, CROSS, SELF.
A: INNER JOIN returns rows that match on both sides — any unmatched rows are dropped. LEFT JOIN keeps all left rows and fills right-side columns with NULL for unmatched. RIGHT JOIN is the mirror. FULL OUTER JOIN keeps everything from both sides with NULLs for the non-matching side. CROSS JOIN produces every combination of rows — rows_left × rows_right — useful for generating combinations but dangerous unintentionally. SELF JOIN joins a table to itself — useful for hierarchical or sequential comparisons within the same table. At HPCL I used LEFT JOIN almost exclusively — joining fill records to plant master and keeping all fill records even if plant metadata was missing, so I could identify data gaps. I used SELF JOIN on the maintenance table to compute time-between-failures per equipment tag.

GROUP: SQL, Oracle & Hive HQL
Q: When do you use a LEFT JOIN instead of an INNER JOIN?
A: I use LEFT JOIN when I want to keep all rows from the left table regardless of whether they match the right side. The signal is that unmatched rows are meaningful — either as data quality flags or as analytical completeness requirements. At HPCL when joining fill records to the approved distributor list, an INNER JOIN would silently drop any fill records for distributors not in the approved list — hiding a data integrity problem. A LEFT JOIN kept those records and showed NULL in the distributor approval columns, making the gap visible. I also default to LEFT JOIN when the right-side table is a dimension or lookup — there's no reason to lose fact records just because a dimension entry is missing or stale.

GROUP: SQL, Oracle & Hive HQL
Q: What is a SELF JOIN and give a real HPCL use case?
A: A SELF JOIN joins a table to itself on different columns or conditions. The most common use is computing a value that requires comparing one row to another row in the same table. At HPCL I used a SELF JOIN on the maintenance records table to compute the time between consecutive maintenance events per equipment tag — SELECT a.equipment_id, a.maintenance_date, b.maintenance_date AS next_maintenance_date, b.maintenance_date - a.maintenance_date AS days_between FROM maintenance_records a JOIN maintenance_records b ON a.equipment_id = b.equipment_id AND b.maintenance_date = (SELECT MIN(c.maintenance_date) FROM maintenance_records c WHERE c.equipment_id = a.equipment_id AND c.maintenance_date > a.maintenance_date). I later replaced this with LEAD() which is cleaner, but the SELF JOIN pattern is worth knowing for databases without window functions.

GROUP: SQL, Oracle & Hive HQL
Q: What is the difference between WHERE and ON in a JOIN — does it matter?
A: For INNER JOINs the result is mathematically identical — both filter the joined result to matching rows. For OUTER JOINs the distinction is critical. A condition in ON is applied during the join and non-matching rows from the preserved side still appear with NULLs. A condition in WHERE is applied after the join and filters out those NULL rows — effectively converting a LEFT JOIN to an INNER JOIN. At HPCL I hit this once when someone added a date filter in WHERE on a LEFT JOIN — they expected all plants to appear in the output even if they had no fills that day, but the WHERE filter dropped all the NULL-fill plants. Moving the date condition to the ON clause fixed it. I always put join conditions in ON and post-join filters in WHERE, and flag any condition in WHERE on the non-preserved side of an outer join as a likely bug.

GROUP: SQL, Oracle & Hive HQL
Q: What causes an unintentional Cartesian product and how do you detect it?
A: A Cartesian product happens when two tables are joined without a join condition, or with a join condition that doesn't actually filter — producing every combination of rows. Common causes: a missing ON clause, joining on a column with very low cardinality like a year or status flag that matches almost every row, or accidentally using CROSS JOIN syntax. Detection: compare the row count of the result to what you'd expect. If a join between a 100-row and a 50-row table produces 5,000 rows instead of roughly 100, you have a Cartesian product. In Oracle the execution plan will show a MERGE JOIN CARTESIAN or HASH JOIN CARTESIAN operation. At HPCL I always run a quick COUNT() on join results and compare to the primary table's count as a sanity check before any downstream aggregation.

GROUP: SQL, Oracle & Hive HQL
Q: What is a HASH JOIN vs a NESTED LOOP join — when does Oracle use each?
A: In a NESTED LOOP join Oracle iterates every row in the outer table and probes an index on the inner table for each one. It's fast when the outer table is small and the inner table has an efficient index — good for OLTP lookups. For a HASH JOIN Oracle builds an in-memory hash table from the smaller input and probes it with each row from the larger input — no index needed. Hash joins are typically faster for large tables and analytics workloads where no index exists on the join column, or when the join returns a large fraction of rows. Oracle's optimizer chooses between them based on table statistics and available memory. In the procurement analytics at HPCL, large-table joins between transaction data and vendor master always resolved to HASH JOIN — which I could verify by reading the execution plan.

GROUP: SQL, Oracle & Hive HQL
Q: How do you read and use an Oracle execution plan?
A: I generate it with EXPLAIN PLAN FOR my_query, then read it with SELECT * FROM TABLE(DBMS_XPLAN.DISPLAY). The plan reads bottom-up — the indented nodes at the bottom execute first. I look for a few things: TABLE ACCESS FULL on large tables is often a problem — it means no index is being used. INDEX RANGE SCAN is good. NESTED LOOPS on large tables with high row counts is usually slow — HASH JOIN is typically better there. The Cardinality column shows Oracle's estimate of rows at each step — a large discrepancy between estimated and actual (visible in the STATISTICS MODE plan) points to stale statistics. At HPCL I used execution plans routinely to diagnose slow procurement queries — most problems traced back to a missing index on a foreign key column or stale table statistics causing a wrong join strategy.

GROUP: SQL, Oracle & Hive HQL
Q: What is an index in Oracle — what types exist and when does each apply?
A: An index is a separate data structure that allows Oracle to find rows without scanning the full table. B-tree index is the default — good for high-cardinality columns, equality and range predicates. Bitmap index is for very low-cardinality columns like status flags or gender — it stores a compressed bit array per value. Bitmap joins are very efficient in data warehouses for dimension lookups. Function-based index indexes the result of a function on a column — essential when queries filter on UPPER(last_name) or TRUNC(fill_date). Composite index covers multiple columns and is most useful when queries filter on the leading columns. At HPCL I created composite indexes on (plant_id, fill_date) on the cylinder fills table and function-based indexes on date expressions used in reporting queries — those two changes cut average report query time by about 70%.

GROUP: SQL, Oracle & Hive HQL
Q: What is a function-based index and why is it important?
A: A regular index on a column is useless if queries filter on that column inside a function — Oracle can't use the index for UPPER(column_name) = 'VALUE' because the indexed value isn't transformed. A function-based index indexes the result of the function itself, so Oracle can use it for exactly that query pattern. At HPCL the equipment maintenance table had a varchar equipment_code column and queries always filtered with UPPER(equipment_code) for case-insensitive lookup — until I added a function-based index on UPPER(equipment_code), every query did a full table scan. Adding the index cut maintenance lookup queries from multi-second to under 100ms. They're also useful for date truncations — indexing TRUNC(maintenance_date, 'MM') when queries commonly filter by month without knowing the exact date.

GROUP: SQL, Oracle & Hive HQL
Q: What causes an index to not be used in Oracle and how do you fix it?
A: Five common causes. Applying a function to the indexed column in the WHERE clause — use a function-based index or rewrite the filter. Leading column not included — a composite index on (plant_id, fill_date) won't be used for a query filtering only on fill_date. Data type mismatch — comparing a VARCHAR2 column to a numeric literal causes Oracle to implicitly convert, defeating the index. Low selectivity — Oracle may prefer a full table scan if the index would return more than roughly 15–20% of rows. Stale statistics — if Oracle thinks the table has 1,000 rows when it has 1 million, it may choose the wrong plan. At HPCL most index misses were the function application problem — legacy reporting queries that used TO_CHAR on date columns instead of direct date comparisons.

GROUP: SQL, Oracle & Hive HQL
Q: What are Oracle hints and when do you use them?
A: Hints are directives embedded in a query comment that override the optimizer's plan — SELECT /*+ INDEX(fills idx_plant_date) */ from fills WHERE.... I use them sparingly and always as a last resort — if the optimizer is making a bad choice, the right fix is usually updating statistics or adding an index, not patching the query with a hint. Hints become a maintenance liability because they're tied to object names and break silently when tables are renamed. Legitimate cases where I've used hints: forcing a FULL hint to avoid an index when I know a full scan is faster for a bulk analytics load, or NO_PARALLEL when a query is accidentally going parallel and causing resource contention. At HPCL I added a USE_NL hint once when a complex join was getting a bad hash join plan due to severely stale stats — fixed the stats the next day and removed the hint.

GROUP: SQL, Oracle & Hive HQL
Q: What is the difference between a primary key and a unique constraint?
A: Both enforce uniqueness. A primary key additionally enforces NOT NULL — it cannot contain NULLs. A unique constraint allows NULLs — and Oracle treats multiple NULLs as not violating uniqueness since NULL is not equal to anything including another NULL. A table can have only one primary key but multiple unique constraints. Oracle automatically creates a unique index to enforce both. At HPCL the spare parts master used a surrogate numeric primary key for internal joins and a unique constraint on the external part number code — which could occasionally be null for custom items. I use primary keys for the row's identity column and unique constraints for natural keys that have real-world uniqueness but might have edge cases with NULLs during data migration or initial setup.

GROUP: SQL, Oracle & Hive HQL
Q: What is statistics gathering in Oracle and why does it matter for the optimizer?
A: Oracle's cost-based optimizer makes execution plan decisions based on statistics about tables and indexes — row count, column cardinality, value distribution histograms, average row size. If those statistics are stale, the optimizer makes bad choices — picking a nested loop when a hash join would be faster, or scanning a full table when an index covers it. I gather statistics using DBMS_STATS.GATHER_TABLE_STATS. At HPCL after any large batch load — monthly SAP procurement imports, quarterly distributor data refreshes — I always ran statistics gathering on the affected tables before running analytical queries. We had one incident where a quarterly load tripled the distributor transaction table and procurement reports went from 3 minutes to 45 minutes overnight — all because the optimizer still thought the table had the old row count.

GROUP: SQL, Oracle & Hive HQL
Q: What is partitioning in Oracle and how does it improve query performance?
A: Oracle partitioning divides a large table into smaller physical segments — partitions — based on column values, while the table appears as a single object to SQL. Range partitioning by date is the most common — each partition holds one month or one year of data. Partition pruning means queries filtering on the partition key only scan relevant partitions, not the full table. At HPCL the cylinder fill records table grew to hundreds of millions of rows over 12 years. Partitioning by fill_date meant any query for a specific date range only touched the relevant monthly partitions — what previously took 3+ minutes on a full table scan dropped under 10 seconds for monthly queries. Partitioning also improves maintenance — purging old data by dropping a partition is near-instant versus a row-by-row DELETE.

GROUP: SQL, Oracle & Hive HQL
Q: What is query rewriting — give three common rewrites that improve performance?
A: First: replacing correlated subqueries with joins or window functions. A correlated subquery re-executes per outer row — for a 1-million-row outer table that's 1 million subquery executions. Rewriting as a JOIN or ROW_NUMBER window function executes once. Second: replacing NOT IN with NOT EXISTS when NULLs are possible — NOT IN with any NULL in the subquery returns no rows silently. NOT EXISTS handles NULLs correctly and typically uses a more efficient plan. Third: avoiding functions on indexed columns in WHERE — rewriting WHERE UPPER(vendor_name) = 'HPCL' to WHERE vendor_name = 'HPCL' or using a function-based index. These three cover roughly 80% of the slow query reports I've investigated across HPCL's Oracle databases over 12 years.

GROUP: SQL, Oracle & Hive HQL
Q: What is ROWNUM in Oracle and what trap must you avoid when using it to get top N rows?
A: ROWNUM assigns a sequential number to rows as they come out of the query — before ORDER BY is applied. The trap: WHERE ROWNUM <= 10 on an unordered query gives you 10 arbitrary rows, not the top 10 by any metric. If you add ORDER BY, Oracle first filters by ROWNUM then sorts — so you get 10 random rows, sorted. The correct pattern is to sort in an inner subquery, then filter by ROWNUM in the outer query: SELECT * FROM (SELECT * FROM fills ORDER BY fill_date DESC) WHERE ROWNUM <= 10. In Oracle 12c+ you can use the cleaner FETCH FIRST 10 ROWS ONLY syntax, which correctly applies to the sorted result. At HPCL I replaced all legacy ROWNUM-based pagination with FETCH FIRST after upgrading to 12c.

GROUP: SQL, Oracle & Hive HQL
Q: What is the DUAL table in Oracle?
A: DUAL is a special one-row, one-column table that Oracle provides for situations where you need a SELECT statement but aren't querying a real table — like evaluating an expression or calling a function. SELECT SYSDATE FROM DUAL returns the current date. SELECT 2 + 2 FROM DUAL returns 4. SELECT my_sequence.NEXTVAL FROM DUAL retrieves the next sequence value. At HPCL I used it constantly for quick value checks during development — verifying date arithmetic, testing function behavior, or checking a sequence value without touching any real data. Modern Oracle doesn't strictly require DUAL for many expressions, but it's been part of Oracle SQL convention for decades and you'll see it in essentially every Oracle codebase.

GROUP: SQL, Oracle & Hive HQL
Q: What is a sequence in Oracle and how did you use it?
A: A sequence is a database object that generates unique incrementing numbers — ideal for surrogate primary keys. You create it once with CREATE SEQUENCE my_seq START WITH 1 INCREMENT BY 1, then reference my_seq.NEXTVAL to get the next value or my_seq.CURRVAL for the last value in the current session. At HPCL I used sequences for all surrogate keys on data model tables — the spare parts master, equipment tags, and distributor performance records all used sequence-generated keys rather than natural keys. The advantage over application-generated IDs is that the database guarantees uniqueness across concurrent sessions without locking. In triggers or insert statements the pattern is: INSERT INTO spare_parts (part_id, ...) VALUES (spare_seq.NEXTVAL, ...).

GROUP: SQL, Oracle & Hive HQL
Q: What is Oracle CONNECT BY for hierarchical queries?
A: CONNECT BY is Oracle's native syntax for traversing hierarchical data stored in a table with a self-referencing parent_id column. SELECT plant_id, plant_name, LEVEL FROM org START WITH parent_id IS NULL CONNECT BY PRIOR plant_id = parent_id. START WITH defines the root, CONNECT BY PRIOR defines the parent-child relationship, and LEVEL gives the depth — 1 for root, 2 for its children, and so on. SYS_CONNECT_BY_PATH builds the full path string from root to current node. At HPCL I used CONNECT BY to traverse the organizational hierarchy — HQ down to zones, regions, and plants — for reports that needed to roll up plant-level data through the full org tree. Recursive CTEs are the ANSI standard alternative, but CONNECT BY is often more readable for pure hierarchy traversal in Oracle.

GROUP: SQL, Oracle & Hive HQL
Q: What is the MERGE statement in Oracle and when do you use it?
A: MERGE performs insert-or-update in a single statement — if the row exists, update it; if not, insert it. Also called an upsert. MERGE INTO target_table t USING source_table s ON (t.id = s.id) WHEN MATCHED THEN UPDATE SET t.col = s.col WHEN NOT MATCHED THEN INSERT (id, col) VALUES (s.id, s.col). At HPCL I used MERGE for the distributor performance scoring table — we refreshed scores monthly. A MERGE against the existing scores table correctly updated distributors who already had records and inserted new ones who had just joined the network, in a single efficient operation. Without MERGE you'd need separate UPDATE and INSERT logic with a NOT EXISTS check, which is slower and more error-prone, especially under concurrent access.

GROUP: SQL, Oracle & Hive HQL
Q: What is LISTAGG in Oracle and give a real example?
A: LISTAGG aggregates multiple row values into a comma-separated string within each group. SELECT plant_id, LISTAGG(equipment_code, ', ') WITHIN GROUP (ORDER BY equipment_code) AS equipment_list FROM equipment GROUP BY plant_id. This gives one row per plant with all its equipment codes concatenated. At HPCL I used LISTAGG in the safety audit reporting — rather than returning one row per audit observation, I'd aggregate all observation codes for a plant into a single cell for the summary report. The important limitation: LISTAGG raises an error if the concatenated result exceeds 4,000 characters. For plants with many observations I added a LENGTH check or used a subquery to limit the count. In Oracle 12.2+ you can add ON OVERFLOW TRUNCATE to handle this gracefully without an error.

GROUP: SQL, Oracle & Hive HQL
Q: What is a materialized view in Oracle vs a regular view?
A: A regular view is just a stored SQL query — it executes fresh every time it's queried. A materialized view stores the result physically on disk and refreshes on a schedule or on demand. For complex aggregations over large tables, a materialized view means the expensive computation runs once at refresh time and subsequent queries hit the precomputed result. At HPCL I built a materialized view for the monthly distributor performance summary — a query joining five tables with 12 months of transaction data that took 8 minutes to run. As a materialized view refreshing nightly, the summary was available in under a second for all downstream reports. The tradeoff is storage for the materialized result and potential staleness between refreshes. Oracle's query rewrite feature can also transparently redirect queries against the base tables to the materialized view.

GROUP: SQL, Oracle & Hive HQL
Q: What is Oracle TO_DATE, TO_CHAR, and date arithmetic?
A: TO_DATE converts a string to an Oracle DATE using a format mask — TO_DATE('2026-04-29', 'YYYY-MM-DD'). TO_CHAR converts a DATE or number to a formatted string — TO_CHAR(fill_date, 'YYYY-MM') extracts the year-month for monthly grouping. Date arithmetic in Oracle is simple — adding an integer to a DATE adds that many days. fill_date + 7 gives seven days later. To work with fractions of a day: fill_date + (1/24) adds one hour. SYSDATE is the current timestamp. At HPCL the most common pattern was extracting month and year for reporting groupings — TO_CHAR(fill_date, 'YYYY-MM') grouped records by month in Oracle, and I used the same pattern in HQL using date_format(fill_date, 'yyyy-MM'). The key trap: never compare TO_CHAR output to date literals — always compare DATE to DATE.

GROUP: SQL, Oracle & Hive HQL
Q: What is NVL, NVL2, and NULLIF in Oracle?
A: NVL(col, replacement) returns replacement if col is NULL, otherwise col — the basic null substitute. NVL2(col, not_null_result, null_result) is more flexible — it returns the second argument if col is not NULL and the third if it is. NULLIF(a, b) returns NULL if a equals b, otherwise returns a — useful for avoiding division-by-zero by writing NULLIF(denominator, 0). At HPCL I used NVL constantly for default values in reporting — NVL(complaint_count, 0) to replace missing complaint data with zero rather than NULL, which would cause SUM to ignore the record. NULLIF I used in the percentage calculations for distributor scoring — NULLIF(total_interactions, 0) in the denominator of rate calculations to return NULL instead of a divide-by-zero error, which I then wrapped in NVL to get a displayable zero.

GROUP: SQL, Oracle & Hive HQL
Q: How does Oracle handle NULLs in comparisons — and why is this a common bug source?
A: NULL is not equal to anything, including NULL itself. NULL = NULL is not true — it evaluates to NULL, which is neither true nor false. This means WHERE column = NULL never returns rows — you must use IS NULL or IS NOT NULL. NOT IN with a subquery is the most dangerous case: if the subquery returns any NULLs, NOT IN returns no rows at all because the comparison to NULL is unknown, making the entire condition unsatisfiable. At HPCL this caused a compliance report to return zero rows for two months — a NOT IN against a vendor list that had a few NULL vendor_ids was silently excluding everything. The fix was switching to NOT EXISTS, which handles NULLs correctly. I now treat NOT IN with a subquery as a code smell and always verify the inner query excludes NULLs if NOT IN must be used.

GROUP: SQL, Oracle & Hive HQL
Q: How does NULL behave in aggregate functions?
A: Aggregate functions like SUM, AVG, COUNT, MAX, MIN all ignore NULLs. SUM of (1, NULL, 3) is 4, not NULL. AVG of (10, NULL, 20) is 15 — the NULL row is excluded from both numerator and denominator. COUNT(*) counts all rows including NULLs; COUNT(column) counts only non-NULL values in that column. The practical implication at HPCL: if 200 out of 6,200 distributors had no complaint data (NULL complaint_count), AVG(complaint_count) averaged over 6,000 records, not 6,200 — which could understate or overstate the metric depending on context. If I wanted to include those distributors as zero-complaint, I'd use AVG(NVL(complaint_count, 0)) to convert NULLs to zeros before aggregating. Being explicit about this choice is important for analytical accuracy.

GROUP: SQL, Oracle & Hive HQL
Q: What is COALESCE and when is it better than NVL?
A: COALESCE returns the first non-NULL value in a list of arguments — COALESCE(a, b, c) returns a if not null, else b if not null, else c. NVL only takes two arguments. COALESCE is ANSI standard, so it works across Oracle, SQL Server, PostgreSQL, and Hive without changes — NVL is Oracle-specific. COALESCE is also more efficient when the fallback is a function call — it short-circuits and only evaluates the function if the first argument is NULL. NVL evaluates both arguments regardless. At HPCL I standardized on COALESCE in all new code because we had analysts who moved between Oracle and Hive environments — COALESCE worked in both without modification. I still see NVL in legacy code everywhere, but all new queries I write use COALESCE.

GROUP: SQL, Oracle & Hive HQL
Q: How do NULLs affect JOIN results?
A: In an INNER JOIN, rows with NULL in the join key are excluded from both sides — NULL = NULL is not true so they never match. In a LEFT JOIN, rows from the left side with NULL in the join key still appear, with NULLs in all right-side columns. At HPCL the distributor master occasionally had NULL region_id for newly added records not yet fully classified. A join to the region dimension on region_id would lose those distributors in an INNER JOIN — they'd simply disappear from any regional report. Using a LEFT JOIN preserved them, showing NULL for all regional attributes, which made the gap visible rather than silently dropping those distributors from analysis. I always audit join result counts against source table counts to catch this class of problem before reports reach stakeholders.

GROUP: SQL, Oracle & Hive HQL
Q: What are ACID properties and what does each mean?
A: Atomicity: a transaction is all-or-nothing — if any part fails, the entire transaction is rolled back. Consistency: a transaction brings the database from one valid state to another, respecting all constraints. Isolation: concurrent transactions don't see each other's intermediate states — the database appears as if transactions ran serially. Durability: once committed, changes survive system failures — they're written to durable storage. At HPCL these properties mattered most in the procurement pipeline: when APES generated an evaluation and wrote to the audit table, atomicity guaranteed that either all records were committed together or none were. If the server crashed mid-write, the entire evaluation was rolled back rather than leaving a partial audit trail, which would have been a compliance violation.

GROUP: SQL, Oracle & Hive HQL
Q: What is Oracle's default isolation level and what problems does it prevent?
A: Oracle's default is READ COMMITTED — a transaction sees only data that was committed before each statement begins. This prevents dirty reads — reading uncommitted changes from another transaction. It doesn't prevent non-repeatable reads — if you read the same row twice in one transaction, another committed transaction could have changed it between your two reads. Oracle also supports SERIALIZABLE isolation, which prevents non-repeatable reads and phantom reads by giving each transaction a consistent snapshot of the database at the transaction start time. Oracle implements this through MVCC — it uses the undo tablespace to reconstruct older versions of data for transactions that need a consistent view without blocking readers. At HPCL for the daily ETL we kept READ COMMITTED since each step read data once.

GROUP: SQL, Oracle & Hive HQL
Q: What is Oracle Multi-Version Concurrency Control (MVCC)?
A: MVCC is Oracle's mechanism for allowing readers and writers to coexist without blocking each other. When a transaction modifies a row, Oracle writes the before-image to the undo tablespace. Other transactions that started before the modification can read the before-image — they see a consistent snapshot of the data as it was when their transaction began. This means a long-running analytics query doesn't block INSERT or UPDATE operations, and INSERT or UPDATE doesn't block readers. At HPCL the daily ETL pipeline ran alongside reporting queries without any explicit locking coordination — MVCC handled consistency transparently. The only side effect: if the undo tablespace is insufficient for very long-running queries, you get an ORA-01555 snapshot too old error — we sized the undo tablespace to accommodate the longest-running overnight analytics jobs.

GROUP: SQL, Oracle & Hive HQL
Q: What is a deadlock and how does Oracle handle it?
A: A deadlock occurs when transaction A holds a lock that transaction B needs, and transaction B holds a lock that transaction A needs — both are waiting for the other and neither can proceed. Oracle detects deadlocks automatically by periodically checking for circular wait chains. When it finds one, it rolls back one of the transactions — typically the one that most recently entered the wait — and raises ORA-00060 to the application. The other transaction can then proceed. At HPCL deadlocks occasionally appeared in the procurement update scripts when multiple concurrent sessions updated different rows in the same table in conflicting orders. The fix was standardizing the order of operations — always lock vendor records before order records, for example — so concurrent transactions always acquired locks in the same order, eliminating the circular dependency.

GROUP: SQL, Oracle & Hive HQL
Q: What is a COMMIT and ROLLBACK and when do you issue them in a pipeline?
A: COMMIT makes all changes from the current transaction permanent and visible to other sessions. ROLLBACK undoes all changes back to the last COMMIT or the transaction start. In a data pipeline the pattern I use is: perform all transformations within a transaction, validate the results — row counts, checksum, constraint checks — then COMMIT if validation passes and ROLLBACK if it fails. At HPCL our nightly ETL had explicit COMMIT points after each logical pipeline stage: after loading staging tables, after applying transformations, after writing to the reporting layer. Each COMMIT was preceded by a count check against the expected row range — if actual rows were outside the expected range, we rolled back that stage and raised an alert rather than committing bad data silently downstream.

GROUP: SQL, Oracle & Hive HQL
Q: What is normalization — explain 1NF, 2NF, 3NF?
A: Normalization eliminates redundancy and ensures data integrity. First Normal Form: each column holds atomic values — no repeating groups or arrays. If I stored all distributor complaint categories in one comma-separated column, that violates 1NF. Second Normal Form: applies to tables with composite primary keys — every non-key column must depend on the entire key, not just part of it. Third Normal Form: no transitive dependencies — non-key columns shouldn't depend on other non-key columns. At HPCL the spare parts master was normalized to 3NF — part description, category, and supplier info lived in separate tables joined by foreign keys, rather than repeating supplier address in every part record. For analytical workloads I then denormalize into a star schema — normalization is for transactional systems, denormalization is for BI.

GROUP: SQL, Oracle & Hive HQL
Q: What is denormalization and when is it appropriate?
A: Denormalization intentionally introduces redundancy — combining columns from multiple tables into one wide table — to improve read performance. Normalized tables require many joins which add latency, especially for analytics over large datasets. In a data warehouse or BI layer, denormalization into a star schema means one fact table with pre-joined dimension attributes — no joins needed in the reporting query. At HPCL the transactional Oracle database was fully normalized. The Power BI data model was fully denormalized — DimPlant had plant_name, zone_name, region_name, state all in one flat table rather than three normalized tables. That's why Power BI reports ran in under a second despite covering 12 years of data — no join overhead at query time. The rule: normalize for writes and data integrity, denormalize for reads and analytics.

GROUP: SQL, Oracle & Hive HQL
Q: What is referential integrity and how did you enforce it in Oracle at HPCL?
A: Referential integrity ensures that foreign key values in a child table always correspond to an existing primary key in the parent table. Enforced via FOREIGN KEY constraints in Oracle — Oracle prevents inserting a child row with a parent_id that doesn't exist, and prevents deleting a parent row that still has child references unless ON DELETE CASCADE is specified. At HPCL I enforced it on the spare parts data model — the purchase order line items table had a foreign key to the spare parts master, preventing orphaned order lines for parts that didn't exist in the master. I also used it on the distributor performance table referencing the distributor master. The one case where I disabled FK constraints was during bulk ETL loads — re-enabling after the load is a common pattern to improve load speed while maintaining data quality checks separately.

GROUP: SQL, Oracle & Hive HQL
Q: What is a star schema vs snowflake schema?
A: A star schema has a central fact table directly connected to fully denormalized dimension tables — DimPlant has all plant attributes in one flat table. A snowflake normalizes those dimension tables further — Plant connects to Zone, Zone connects to Region, rather than having all three in one table. Star schema requires more storage — zone name is repeated for every plant — but query performance is better because no dimension-join chains are needed. Snowflake saves storage but adds join complexity. For Power BI and analytics workloads I always use star schema. At HPCL I initially built a snowflake model and measured 2.3-second report load times. After flattening the dimension tables into star, load time dropped to 0.8 seconds — the VertiPaq engine compresses column redundancy extremely well, making the storage argument for snowflake irrelevant in practice.

GROUP: SQL, Oracle & Hive HQL
Q: What are Slowly Changing Dimensions and what types did you implement?
A: SCDs track how dimension attributes change over time. Type 1 overwrites the old value — no history kept. Type 2 adds a new row with effective dates and an is_current flag, preserving full history. Type 3 adds a previous-value column, keeping only one prior state. At HPCL I implemented Type 2 for the distributor performance tier — gold, silver, bronze changes quarterly. When a distributor moved from silver to gold I inserted a new row with the new tier and start date, and set the old row's end date. This let me answer "what tier was distributor X in Q3 FY2022" using the tier active during that period, not the current tier. Without Type 2, historical analysis of tier-correlated metrics would be corrupted by applying today's classifications to historical periods.

GROUP: SQL, Oracle & Hive HQL
Q: What is a surrogate key and why is it preferred over a natural key?
A: A surrogate key is a system-generated identifier — typically a sequence number — with no business meaning. A natural key uses a real-world identifier like part number or employee ID. Surrogate keys are preferred because natural keys change. Vendor part numbers get updated. Distributor codes get reassigned. When a natural key changes, every child record referencing it requires an update — cascading updates that can be expensive and error-prone. A surrogate key never changes — the business identifier can change freely while the internal join key remains stable. At HPCL I used surrogate keys for all data model tables — the spare parts master had an internal part_id sequence number as the primary key and the external manufacturer_part_number as a unique constraint. The constraint catches duplicates; the surrogate key handles joins.

GROUP: SQL, Oracle & Hive HQL
Q: Walk me through the SQL logic of the $2.3M procurement savings analysis.
A: The analysis had four logical stages in Oracle SQL. First: join three years of purchase order line items from SAP to the purchasing info record table — PIR holds the negotiated reference price for each vendor-material combination. Second: compute price_variance = actual_PO_price - PIR_reference_price per line item. Third: aggregate by spare category across all 55 plants — GROUP BY category, SUM(price_variance * quantity) to get total excess spend per category over three years. Fourth: rank categories by total excess spend and filter to the top contributors. Categories where average PO price was consistently 8–15% above PIR indicated either outdated reference prices or unauthorized buyer overrides — those 7 categories accounted for the $2.3M in verified annual savings.

GROUP: SQL, Oracle & Hive HQL
Q: How did you compute purchase price variance across 30-40 spare categories in Oracle/SQL?
A: The core query joined the PO line items table on vendor_id and material_code to the purchasing info record table to get the PIR reference price alongside the actual PO price. I used a CASE WHEN to classify each line item into one of the 30-40 spare categories based on the material group code. Then groupby category, compute average actual price and average PIR price, compute variance as a percentage, and rank by total variance value. The final output flagged categories where average PO price exceeded PIR by more than 5% — those were escalated to the procurement head with the list of specific POs and officers involved. The vendor-material level granularity was essential — aggregate averages would have hidden that overrides were concentrated in a handful of vendor-buyer pairs.

GROUP: SQL, Oracle & Hive HQL
Q: How did you design the Oracle data model for equipment maintenance at HPCL?
A: Three core tables. Equipment master: equipment_id as surrogate key, equipment_code, equipment_type, plant_id foreign key, manufacturer, install_date, decommission_date. Maintenance events: event_id surrogate key, equipment_id foreign key, maintenance_type, event_date, technician_id, work_order_reference, labor_hours, material_cost. Spare parts consumption: consumption_id, event_id foreign key, part_id foreign key, quantity, unit_cost. This structure let me compute MTBF per equipment class by joining consecutive maintenance events on equipment_id using LAG. Total cost per event came from summing labor cost (hours × rate) plus material cost from the consumption table. The equipment master's install_date and decommission_date let me compute age at time of maintenance, which was a key feature in the predictive maintenance model.

GROUP: SQL, Oracle & Hive HQL
Q: How did you identify duplicate records in SCADA retransmissions using ROW_NUMBER?
A: SCADA occasionally retransmitted fill records on network interruptions — the same physical fill event would appear 2 or 3 times in the landing table with slightly different received_timestamps but identical fill_timestamp and equipment_id. The deduplication query: SELECT * FROM (SELECT *, ROW_NUMBER() OVER (PARTITION BY equipment_id, fill_timestamp ORDER BY received_timestamp ASC) AS rn FROM cylinder_fills_landing) WHERE rn = 1. Partitioning by equipment_id and fill_timestamp groups all duplicates of the same physical fill event together. Ordering by received_timestamp ascending and keeping rn = 1 retains the earliest received copy, which was the most reliable in our experience. This ran as the first transformation in the daily ETL before any downstream aggregation.

GROUP: SQL, Oracle & Hive HQL
Q: Write the logic for the top N distributor ranking query using window functions.
A: SELECT distributor_id, distributor_name, annual_score, region_id, DENSE_RANK() OVER (PARTITION BY region_id ORDER BY annual_score DESC) AS regional_rank, DENSE_RANK() OVER (ORDER BY annual_score DESC) AS national_rank, NTILE(10) OVER (ORDER BY annual_score DESC) AS national_decile FROM distributor_annual_scores WHERE fiscal_year = 2025. To get only the top 10 per region, wrap this in an outer query and filter WHERE regional_rank <= 10. I used DENSE_RANK over RANK so tied scores don't create gaps in the ranking, and NTILE(10) alongside it for decile analysis. This query was the foundation of the monthly distributor performance report — regional managers saw their top performers, national teams saw the full distribution.

GROUP: SQL, Oracle & Hive HQL
Q: How do you calculate year-over-year growth percentage in SQL?
A: Using LAG with a 12-month offset for monthly data, or a 1-year offset for annual data. For monthly: SELECT plant_id, fill_month, total_volume, LAG(total_volume, 12) OVER (PARTITION BY plant_id ORDER BY fill_month) AS volume_prior_year, ROUND((total_volume - LAG(total_volume, 12) OVER (PARTITION BY plant_id ORDER BY fill_month)) / NULLIF(LAG(total_volume, 12) OVER (PARTITION BY plant_id ORDER BY fill_month), 0) * 100, 2) AS yoy_growth_pct FROM monthly_plant_volume. The NULLIF in the denominator prevents division-by-zero when prior year volume was zero. At HPCL this appeared in nearly every executive report — plant managers always wanted to see current month's volume versus same month last year.

GROUP: SQL, Oracle & Hive HQL
Q: How do you pivot rows into columns in Oracle SQL?
A: Oracle's PIVOT clause handles this natively. SELECT * FROM (SELECT plant_id, fill_month, total_volume FROM monthly_fills) PIVOT (SUM(total_volume) FOR fill_month IN ('2025-01' AS jan, '2025-02' AS feb, '2025-03' AS mar, ...)) ORDER BY plant_id. Each value in the FOR clause becomes a column. The limitation is that IN values must be known at query write time — for dynamic month columns you'd need dynamic SQL with DBMS_SQL or EXECUTE IMMEDIATE. I used static PIVOT for fixed reporting periods — 12 months of a fiscal year — where the columns were predetermined. For ad-hoc period pivots in Power BI I did the pivot in Power Query M instead, which handles dynamic column sets more cleanly than Oracle's PIVOT syntax.

GROUP: SQL, Oracle & Hive HQL
Q: How do you find the top N records per group in SQL without a subquery per row?
A: The efficient pattern is ROW_NUMBER in a subquery, then filter in the outer query — no correlated subquery per row. SELECT * FROM (SELECT plant_id, fill_date, fill_volume, ROW_NUMBER() OVER (PARTITION BY plant_id ORDER BY fill_volume DESC) AS rn FROM cylinder_fills WHERE fill_year = 2025) WHERE rn <= 5. This gives the 5 highest fill volumes per plant. The window function executes once over the full dataset, and the outer filter just picks rows where rn is within range. Using a correlated subquery to find the top N per group — SELECT * FROM fills f WHERE fill_volume IN (SELECT TOP 5 fill_volume FROM fills WHERE plant_id = f.plant_id ORDER BY fill_volume DESC) — executes a subquery for every row in the outer table, which is catastrophically slow at scale.

GROUP: SQL, Oracle & Hive HQL
Q: What is PL/SQL and how is it different from SQL?
A: SQL is a declarative language for querying and manipulating data — you describe what you want, not how to get it. PL/SQL is Oracle's procedural extension — it adds variables, loops, conditionals, exception handling, and the ability to call SQL statements within a procedural block. It runs inside the database engine, so it avoids the round-trip overhead of executing SQL from an application repeatedly. At HPCL I used PL/SQL for the procurement validation triggers and the nightly ETL batch procedures that sequenced multiple transformation steps with conditional logic — check row counts, if within range proceed, else log error and exit. I've mostly moved toward Python for orchestration now because it's more testable and version-controllable, but PL/SQL remains valuable for logic that needs to run close to the data with minimal network overhead.

GROUP: SQL, Oracle & Hive HQL
Q: What is the difference between TRUNCATE and DELETE in Oracle?
A: DELETE removes rows based on a WHERE clause — or all rows if no WHERE — and is a DML operation, so it's transactional and can be rolled back. TRUNCATE removes all rows, is a DDL operation, and cannot be rolled back — it implicitly commits. TRUNCATE is much faster because it doesn't log individual row deletions — it resets the high water mark of the table. At HPCL I used TRUNCATE for staging tables at the start of each ETL run — TRUNCATE staging_table followed by INSERT from source. This was safe because staging tables hold temporary data that's reconstructed each run. For selective row deletion — purging records older than a certain date — DELETE with a WHERE clause is the only option. Accidental TRUNCATE on a production table without a backup is one of the most severe DBA incidents; we had strict access controls on it.

GROUP: SQL, Oracle & Hive HQL
Q: How do you handle large batch updates in Oracle without locking issues?
A: Two approaches. First: process in chunks — instead of one giant UPDATE that locks the entire table for hours, update 10,000 rows at a time and COMMIT after each chunk. This releases locks frequently, lets other sessions access the table, and reduces undo tablespace pressure. Second: use SELECT ... FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED to avoid waiting on rows already locked by other sessions — useful when multiple processes update different subsets of the same table. At HPCL the quarterly distributor score update touched 6,200 rows — one UPDATE and COMMIT was fine. The spare parts reorder point recalculation across all 55 plants updated 1,500+ SKUs and ran in batches of 200 with intermediate COMMITs to keep the ETL window predictable and avoid ORA-01555 snapshot too old errors on concurrent reporting queries.

GROUP: SQL, Oracle & Hive HQL
Q: What is Oracle ROWID and when is it useful?
A: ROWID is a pseudocolumn that represents the physical address of a row — its exact location in the database including the data file, block, and row within the block. It's the fastest way to access a specific row because it bypasses index lookup entirely. The main practical use is efficient delete or update of specific rows found by a query. In deduplication: SELECT MIN(ROWID), equipment_id, fill_timestamp FROM cylinder_fills GROUP BY equipment_id, fill_timestamp HAVING COUNT(*) > 1 — this gives you the ROWID of the row to keep, then DELETE FROM cylinder_fills WHERE ROWID NOT IN (the kept ROWIDs). This is faster than re-running the full deduplication query in the WHERE clause. ROWID is specific to Oracle and should never be stored or relied upon across transactions — it can change during table reorganization.

GROUP: SQL, Oracle & Hive HQL
Q: What is the EXPLAIN command in Hive and what do you look for?
A: EXPLAIN shows the execution plan for a HQL query without running it — useful for understanding how Hive will process the query and where the bottlenecks might be. EXPLAIN EXTENDED adds more detail about the stage plan. What I look for: the number of MapReduce or Tez stages — more stages usually means more shuffle operations. Whether predicate pushdown is happening — filters should appear in the scan stage, not after a full table scan. Whether partition pruning is active — the plan should show which partitions will be read based on filter conditions. At HPCL I used EXPLAIN to verify that plant_id and date filters were actually pruning partitions before running queries on the full 12-year dataset. If the plan showed a full table scan despite a date filter, it usually meant the filter was on a non-partitioned column or had a function applied that defeated pruning.

GROUP: SQL, Oracle & Hive HQL
Q: What are SORT BY, ORDER BY, DISTRIBUTE BY, and CLUSTER BY in HQL and how do they differ?
A: ORDER BY guarantees a globally sorted result — it forces all data through a single reducer, which can be slow for large datasets. SORT BY sorts within each reducer independently — results are locally sorted but not globally ordered across reducers. DISTRIBUTE BY controls which rows go to which reducer based on a column value — all rows with the same plant_id go to the same reducer. CLUSTER BY is shorthand for DISTRIBUTE BY and SORT BY on the same column — it distributes data to reducers and sorts within each reducer. At HPCL I used DISTRIBUTE BY plant_id with SORT BY fill_date when generating per-plant output files — ensuring each plant's data was directed to one reducer and sorted by date within it, without the cost of a global ORDER BY sort across all 55 plants.

GROUP: SQL, Oracle & Hive HQL
Q: What is a lateral view and explode in HQL and when do you use them?
A: EXPLODE is a Hive table-generating function — it takes an array or map column and emits one row per element. LATERAL VIEW connects the exploded result back to the original row. For example: if each distributor record has an array of complaint_categories, SELECT distributor_id, category FROM distributors LATERAL VIEW EXPLODE(complaint_categories) cat_table AS category turns each array element into its own row while preserving the distributor_id. At HPCL I used this pattern when ingesting distributor feedback JSON where each feedback record contained an array of tagged topics. Exploding the array into individual rows allowed me to group by topic and compute sentiment aggregations per topic type — which fed the aspect-based sentiment analysis platform. Without LATERAL VIEW EXPLODE, working with array columns in Hive requires complex UDFs or post-processing.

GROUP: SQL, Oracle & Hive HQL
Q: What is a map-side join in Hive and when do you use it?
A: A map-side join — also called a broadcast join in Hive — broadcasts the smaller table to every mapper node, allowing the join to happen during the map phase without a shuffle or reducer. The result is a single-stage MapReduce job instead of a two-stage one with a shuffle. In HQL you enable it with SET hive.auto.convert.join=true and configure the table size threshold — any table under hive.mapjoin.smalltable.filesize bytes gets broadcast automatically. At HPCL I explicitly configured this for joins between the large fill records table and the small plant master or equipment type tables — both were well under 50 MB. The fill records table was hundreds of gigabytes; the map-side join eliminated the full shuffle and cut those queries from 25 minutes to under 8.

GROUP: SQL, Oracle & Hive HQL
Q: What is a skewed join in Hive and how do you handle it?
A: A skewed join in Hive occurs when one or a few join key values have far more rows than the rest — those reducers get most of the data while others sit idle. Hive has a built-in skewed join optimization via hive.optimize.skewjoin=true and hive.skewjoin.key — when Hive detects that a key appears more than the threshold number of times, it separates those rows and processes them via a broadcast join, then unions with the non-skewed result. At HPCL our plant_code join had heavy skew — three high-volume plants accounted for 60% of fill records. Enabling hive.optimize.skewjoin automatically handled those plants via broadcast, cutting the query runtime significantly. I also manually added a WHERE filter to exclude the skewed keys, join separately on a smaller dataset, and UNION ALL — the manual approach when the automatic optimization wasn't tuning aggressively enough.

GROUP: SQL, Oracle & Hive HQL
Q: How do you tune the number of reducers in a Hive query?
A: Hive calculates the default reducer count from the total input data size divided by hive.exec.reducers.bytes.per.reducer — defaulting to 256 MB per reducer. Too few reducers means each reducer handles too much data. Too many means scheduling overhead dominates for small outputs. I tune it two ways: globally via SET hive.exec.reducers.bytes.per.reducer to adjust the per-reducer target, or explicitly via SET mapreduce.job.reduces = N for specific queries where I know the right count. At HPCL the monthly distributor aggregation query produced about 2 GB of intermediate shuffle data — the default of 256 MB per reducer would have created 8 reducers, which was appropriate. For the full 12-year historical analysis producing 50 GB of shuffle data I increased the reducer count to 50 to speed up the aggregation stage.

GROUP: SQL, Oracle & Hive HQL
Q: What is dynamic partitioning in Hive and what are its risks?
A: Dynamic partitioning lets Hive determine partition values from the data at write time rather than specifying them explicitly in the INSERT statement. INSERT INTO TABLE fills PARTITION (fill_date, plant_id) SELECT ..., fill_date, plant_id FROM staging. Hive reads fill_date and plant_id from each row and routes it to the correct partition automatically. The risks: if fill_date has high cardinality — thousands of distinct dates — Hive tries to create thousands of partitions simultaneously, overwhelming the Metastore with concurrent writes. The limit is hive.exec.max.dynamic.partitions — exceeding it fails the job. At HPCL I set hive.exec.dynamic.partition.mode=nonstrict (required to allow all partitions to be dynamic) and carefully bounded partition cardinality — never more than 365 date partitions per load batch, and always partitioned at a coarse granularity like YYYY-MM rather than daily.

GROUP: SQL, Oracle & Hive HQL
Q: What is ACID in Hive and when do you need it?
A: Hive ACID (Atomicity, Consistency, Isolation, Durability) enables row-level INSERT, UPDATE, DELETE, and MERGE operations on Hive tables — standard Hive without ACID only supports bulk INSERT OVERWRITE. It requires ORC format, bucketing, and the transactional=true table property. At HPCL I used ACID for the distributor performance scores table — quarterly updates modified specific distributor records in place rather than full table rewrites. Without ACID the only option would be INSERT OVERWRITE of the entire table, which is expensive and causes the table to be unavailable during the overwrite. The tradeoff: ACID tables have overhead from delta file management and require periodic compaction to merge delta files back into the base. For most analytical tables at HPCL that were append-only, regular external tables with INSERT OVERWRITE for partition rewrites were simpler and faster.

GROUP: SQL, Oracle & Hive HQL
Q: What is the difference between INSERT OVERWRITE and INSERT INTO in Hive?
A: INSERT OVERWRITE replaces all data in the specified partition or table — anything that was there before is deleted and replaced with the new data. INSERT INTO appends to existing data. For idempotent ETL pipelines, INSERT OVERWRITE is strongly preferred — rerunning the job produces the same result regardless of how many times it runs, because each run replaces the partition cleanly. INSERT INTO accumulates data, so if a job reruns due to a failure, you get duplicate records. At HPCL every ETL job that wrote to Hive used INSERT OVERWRITE PARTITION (fill_date = '2026-04-29') — the daily partition was replaced atomically. If the job failed midway, the previous day's data remained intact. INSERT INTO was reserved for append-only audit logging tables where accumulation was the intended behavior.

GROUP: SQL, Oracle & Hive HQL
Q: What is MSCK REPAIR TABLE in Hive and when do you use it?
A: MSCK REPAIR TABLE scans the HDFS directory structure for a partitioned table and registers any partitions that exist on disk but aren't known to the Hive Metastore. When you add partitions to HDFS directly — via a Spark job, a file copy, or an external process — without going through Hive's ALTER TABLE ADD PARTITION, the Metastore doesn't know those partitions exist. Queries that reference them get no results. MSCK REPAIR TABLE finds all unregistered partitions and adds them to the Metastore in one operation. At HPCL the daily ETL wrote partitions via Spark's write API rather than HQL — so after each daily run I ran MSCK REPAIR TABLE fills to register that day's new partition. It's slower than ALTER TABLE ADD PARTITION (specific partition) because it scans the entire directory tree, so on large tables I used the explicit ADD PARTITION form.

GROUP: SQL, Oracle & Hive HQL
Q: How do you optimize a full table scan in Hive?
A: First, check if partitioning is set up correctly and the query filter uses the partition column — that's the highest-impact fix. If partitioning is in place and filters use the partition key, partition pruning eliminates most of the scan. If not, add appropriate partitioning. Second, enable predicate pushdown — SET hive.optimize.ppd=true — which pushes filter conditions into the ORC reader, allowing it to skip entire row groups using min/max statistics and bloom filters. Third, convert to ORC format if the table is still text or Parquet — ORC's column-level statistics and compression dramatically reduce the data read. Fourth, enable vectorization — SET hive.vectorized.execution.enabled=true — for faster batch-level processing. At HPCL combining partitioning with ORC and predicate pushdown reduced a 3-hour full scan to under 20 minutes on the historical cylinder fills table.

GROUP: SQL, Oracle & Hive HQL
Q: How do you write a conditional expression in HQL?
A: Two ways. CASE WHEN is the ANSI standard and most readable: CASE WHEN fill_volume > 14.2 THEN 'overfill' WHEN fill_volume < 13.8 THEN 'underfill' ELSE 'normal' END AS fill_status. The IF function is Hive-specific and works for simple binary conditions: IF(fill_volume > 14.2, 'overfill', 'normal'). For null handling there's also NVL which doesn't exist in Hive — use COALESCE or NVL2 or IF(col IS NULL, default, col). At HPCL CASE WHEN appeared in almost every analytical query — classifying fill deviations into categories, routing distributor performance scores into tiers, flagging equipment alerts as critical or warning based on sensor thresholds. I use CASE WHEN by default for readability and portability across SQL dialects, reserving IF for simple two-way conditions in HQL where brevity matters.

GROUP: SQL, Oracle & Hive HQL
Q: What is the WHERE vs HAVING distinction in HQL?
A: WHERE filters rows before grouping — it applies to individual rows in the raw data. HAVING filters after grouping — it applies to aggregated results. The practical rule: if the condition references an aggregate function like COUNT, SUM, AVG, it must go in HAVING. If it references an individual row column, put it in WHERE. Putting filters in WHERE rather than HAVING wherever possible improves performance — WHERE reduces the data volume before grouping, while HAVING filters after the expensive groupby operation. At HPCL: WHERE plant_status = 'active' filtered plants before the groupBy, reducing the data Hive had to aggregate. HAVING COUNT(fill_events) > 100 filtered the grouped result to only plants with meaningful fill volumes. Mixing these up — filtering aggregated results in WHERE — produces an error; filtering individual row conditions in HAVING works but wastes compute.
GROUP: Hadoop, HDFS & Hive Architecture
Q: What is HDFS and how does it store data — blocks, NameNode, DataNodes?
A: HDFS splits large files into 128 MB blocks and distributes those blocks across DataNode machines in the cluster. Each block is replicated three times by default across different nodes for fault tolerance. The NameNode is the master — it holds all filesystem metadata: file names, block locations, replication info. It doesn't store actual data. DataNodes are the workers — they hold the blocks on local disk and report to the NameNode. At HPCL every SCADA sensor reading from our 55-plant LPG network landed in HDFS first, then we put Hive external tables on top to query it. The design is intentionally optimized for large sequential reads, not random access.

GROUP: Hadoop, HDFS & Hive Architecture
Q: What is the NameNode and what happens if it goes down?
A: The NameNode is the brain of HDFS — it holds all filesystem metadata including file names, block locations, and replication status, but holds no actual data. If it goes down, the cluster becomes completely unavailable — no client can locate any data block even though the data physically still exists on DataNodes. This was HDFS's original single point of failure. The solution is High Availability NameNode: an active NameNode and a standby NameNode kept synchronized through shared edit logs via JournalNodes. If the active fails, the standby takes over automatically within seconds. At HPCL our NameNode HA configuration was our primary operational concern — we sized the JournalNodes conservatively and had runbooks for manual failover in case automatic failover didn't trigger.

GROUP: Hadoop, HDFS & Hive Architecture
Q: What is a DataNode and what does it do — heartbeat, block report, auto-replication?
A: DataNodes are the worker machines that physically store data blocks on local disk. They send heartbeats to the NameNode every 3 seconds to signal they're alive, plus periodic full block reports listing every block they hold. If the NameNode stops receiving heartbeats from a DataNode after a timeout, it marks that node as dead and automatically triggers re-replication — finding the blocks that were on the failed node and making new copies on healthy nodes to restore the replication factor. At HPCL when a DataNode went offline — usually for hardware maintenance — the cluster self-healed automatically. We'd see re-replication alerts in monitoring and verify block health was restored before bringing the node back up.

GROUP: Hadoop, HDFS & Hive Architecture
Q: What is the default HDFS block size and why is it 128MB vs 4KB in traditional filesystems?
A: 128 MB is the default in modern Hadoop. The large size is deliberate — HDFS is built for sequential reads of very large files, not small random-access operations. Larger blocks mean fewer blocks per file, which reduces the NameNode's metadata overhead significantly — the NameNode holds all block metadata in memory, so fewer blocks means lower RAM requirements. It also means fewer disk seeks since data within a block is read sequentially. Traditional filesystems use 4 KB blocks because they serve small files and random access patterns — very different workloads. At HPCL our daily SCADA sensor files were gigabytes in size, so 128 MB blocks were a natural fit and minimized the number of block entries the NameNode had to track.

GROUP: Hadoop, HDFS & Hive Architecture
Q: What is HDFS replication and how do you configure it — globally and per-file?
A: HDFS stores multiple copies of each data block across different DataNodes. The default replication factor is 3 — one primary copy and two replicas. Configure globally via dfs.replication in hdfs-site.xml. Override per file using hadoop fs -setrep N /path/to/file or at creation time in the client API. HDFS uses rack-aware placement for replica positioning: first replica on the local node, second on a different rack, third on another node in that second rack. This protects against both node failure and full rack failure. At HPCL we kept the default factor of 3 for production operational data. For intermediate staging data that could be easily regenerated from SAP sources, I occasionally reduced to 2 to save storage costs on the cluster.

GROUP: Hadoop, HDFS & Hive Architecture
Q: What is the small files problem in HDFS and how did you handle it at HPCL?
A: Each file in HDFS — regardless of its size — consumes approximately 150 bytes of NameNode heap memory for metadata. So 10 million small files consume 1.5 GB of NameNode RAM just for metadata, before storing a single byte of actual data. Small files also hurt query performance — Hive creates one map task per file, so thousands of tiny files create thousands of near-empty map tasks with more scheduling overhead than actual work. At HPCL our IoT sensors emitted hourly files throughout the day — 24 small files per plant per day times 55 plants. I built nightly compaction jobs that merged all hourly files for each plant-date into one large ORC file using INSERT OVERWRITE, reducing file count by 24x while simultaneously converting from text to the more efficient ORC format.

GROUP: Hadoop, HDFS & Hive Architecture
