Q: What is the difference between HDFS and a traditional relational database?
A: HDFS is a distributed file system — it stores raw files across commodity hardware with schema-on-read, meaning structure is only applied when you query the data, not when you write it. A traditional RDBMS stores structured data in tables with strict schema enforcement, ACID transactions, and row-level DML operations. HDFS excels at sequential batch reads of massive datasets. RDBMS excels at low-latency random reads and writes for transactional workloads. HDFS has no concept of a row — it just stores bytes organized in blocks. RDBMS has no concept of a distributed file — it manages storage internally. At HPCL I used Oracle for the transactional procurement and maintenance systems, and HDFS with Hive on top for the large-scale analytical workloads where the data volume exceeded what Oracle could handle cost-effectively.

GROUP: Hadoop, HDFS & Hive Architecture
Q: What is rack awareness in HDFS and why does it matter?
A: Rack awareness means HDFS knows which DataNodes are on which physical server rack and uses that topology information for replica placement. For a replication factor of 3, HDFS places: first replica on the local node where the write happened, second replica on a different rack, third replica on a different node in that same second rack. This strategy means losing an entire rack — power failure, switch failure, network partition — still leaves at least one copy of every block on the surviving rack. Without rack awareness, all three replicas could end up on the same rack and a single rack failure would cause data loss. At HPCL we configured rack topology for our cluster with each rack hosting 6–8 DataNodes, and verified rack placement using the HDFS fsck command after any new node additions.

GROUP: Hadoop, HDFS & Hive Architecture
Q: What is HDFS Federation and when would you use it?
A: HDFS Federation allows multiple independent NameNodes to share the same set of DataNodes, each managing its own namespace. Each NameNode controls a separate portion of the filesystem tree, and DataNodes register with all NameNodes to serve all namespaces. You'd use it when a single NameNode's memory capacity becomes the bottleneck — with billions of files and blocks, 150 bytes per block adds up. Federation lets you horizontally scale the namespace by adding more NameNodes, each handling a different directory subtree. At HPCL our cluster didn't reach the scale where Federation was necessary — one NameNode with HA was sufficient. Federation becomes relevant at very large organizations with tens of billions of files across multiple departments sharing the same physical cluster.

GROUP: Hadoop, HDFS & Hive Architecture
Q: How do you interact with HDFS from the command line?
A: The primary interface is the hadoop fs command — hadoop fs -ls /user/hpcl lists directory contents, hadoop fs -put local_file.csv /user/hpcl/data/ uploads a file, hadoop fs -get /user/hpcl/data/file.csv ./downloads a file to local. hadoop fs -mkdir creates directories, -rm removes files, -du shows disk usage, -count gives file and directory counts. For checking the health of files and blocks I use hdfs fsck /path -files -blocks -locations — this shows block replication status and can identify under-replicated or corrupt blocks. At HPCL I used hdfs dfs -du -h /user/hpcl/fills/date=2025-* regularly to monitor how much storage the daily fill data was consuming and whether compaction was keeping pace with growth.

GROUP: Hadoop, HDFS & Hive Architecture
Q: How does HDFS handle a DataNode failure during a write operation?
A: HDFS writes use a pipeline — data flows through a chain of DataNodes simultaneously as it's being written. If a DataNode in the pipeline fails during a write, HDFS recovers by: removing the failed node from the pipeline, establishing a new pipeline with the remaining healthy nodes, and completing the write. The block is then flagged as under-replicated since it now has fewer copies than the target replication factor. The NameNode schedules re-replication — finding a healthy DataNode to receive a new copy — until the target replication factor is restored. The client's write completes successfully as long as one DataNode in the pipeline remains healthy. At HPCL we never had a data loss incident from DataNode failures during write operations because the pipeline recovery worked transparently.

GROUP: Hadoop, HDFS & Hive Architecture
Q: What is the SecondaryNameNode — is it actually a backup NameNode?
A: Despite the misleading name, the SecondaryNameNode is not a backup and cannot take over if the primary NameNode fails. It's a checkpoint assistant — it periodically downloads the NameNode's edit log and filesystem image, merges them into a new checkpoint, and uploads it back. This prevents the edit log from growing indefinitely and reduces NameNode recovery time after a restart. In an HA NameNode setup with a standby node, the SecondaryNameNode is typically not used at all — the standby continuously replays edit logs from JournalNodes and can perform checkpointing itself. At HPCL our cluster used HA NameNode, so the SecondaryNameNode was not part of the architecture. The confusion between Secondary NameNode and standby NameNode is one of the most common Hadoop interview misconceptions I've seen.

GROUP: Hadoop, HDFS & Hive Architecture
Q: Explain MapReduce in simple terms with a real HPCL example.
A: MapReduce splits a large computation across many machines in two phases. The Map phase divides the problem — each mapper processes one HDFS block independently and emits key-value pairs. The Reduce phase consolidates results — reducers receive all values for each key from all mappers and produce the final output. A real HPCL example: computing total cylinder volume per plant per day from raw fill records. The Map phase reads fill records and emits (plant_id + date, fill_volume) pairs. The Shuffle phase groups all pairs with the same key to the same reducer. The Reduce phase sums all fill volumes for each plant-date combination. The result is one row per plant per day with total volume — computed across the entire cluster in parallel without any single machine seeing all the data.

GROUP: Hadoop, HDFS & Hive Architecture
Q: What is the Shuffle phase in MapReduce and why is it expensive?
A: The Shuffle phase happens between Map and Reduce — it sorts and transfers the map output to the correct reducers based on key. Each mapper writes its output to local disk sorted by key. The framework then reads those sorted files, merges them, and transfers each key's data across the network to the reducer responsible for that key. The expense comes from: disk I/O at the mapper output, network transfer across the cluster, and merge-sort operations on both sides. For large datasets with many keys this can dominate total job runtime. At HPCL demand analytics jobs that aggregated 12 months of fill data showed shuffle read and write sizes in the hundreds of GB — the Shuffle phase alone accounted for 60% of total job time. Reducing what went into the shuffle — through filtering, projection, and Combiners — was the main optimization lever.

GROUP: Hadoop, HDFS & Hive Architecture
Q: What is a Combiner in MapReduce and when do you use it?
A: A Combiner is a mini-reducer that runs on each mapper's local output before it goes over the network — it reduces the data volume that needs to be shuffled. For associative and commutative operations like SUM, COUNT, MIN, and MAX, the Combiner can partially aggregate the mapper's output before it's sent to the final Reducer. Instead of sending every (plant_id, fill_volume) pair across the network, the Combiner emits partial sums per plant_id per mapper — dramatically reducing shuffle volume. At HPCL on our daily demand aggregation jobs, enabling a SUM combiner cut shuffle data volume by roughly 80% — each mapper typically had thousands of records per plant but the combiner reduced those to one row per plant before the network transfer.

GROUP: Hadoop, HDFS & Hive Architecture
Q: What is a Partitioner in MapReduce?
A: The Partitioner determines which reducer receives a given key-value pair from the map output. The default HashPartitioner hashes the key and assigns it to a reducer based on the hash modulo the number of reducers — ensuring all values for the same key go to the same reducer. Custom Partitioners let you control this distribution. At HPCL if I needed all records from one zone's plants to go to the same reducer — for zone-level aggregation — a custom Partitioner on the zone_id key rather than the plant_id key would achieve that. Custom Partitioners are also the fix for skew when one key dominates: you can add a salt to the key in the Mapper and a corresponding custom Partitioner that routes salted keys to different reducers.

GROUP: Hadoop, HDFS & Hive Architecture
Q: What is data skew in MapReduce and how do you handle it?
A: Data skew in MapReduce occurs when one or a few reducer keys receive far more values than others — those reducers run for much longer than the rest, becoming the bottleneck. In the demand analytics job at HPCL, three high-volume plants contributed 60% of all cylinder fill records — a GROUP BY plant_id aggregation would give those reducers enormous input while others finished quickly. The remedies: add a random salt to the skewed key in the Mapper (plant_id becomes plant_id_3 for a random suffix), aggregate partially on the salted key in a first pass, then aggregate the partial results on the original key in a second pass. Alternatively switch to Hive with hive.optimize.skewjoin enabled, which handles this automatically. In Hive on Tez or Spark, the engine's adaptive optimizations handle most skew without manual intervention.

GROUP: Hadoop, HDFS & Hive Architecture
Q: How does Hive convert HQL to MapReduce — what is the execution translation?
A: When you submit an HQL query, Hive's Driver passes it to the Compiler, which parses the SQL, resolves table and column references against the Metastore, and generates a logical query plan. The optimizer applies rules to the logical plan — predicate pushdown, column pruning, join reordering. The final optimized plan is converted to a physical plan consisting of MapReduce stages — each stage is a MapReduce job. A simple SELECT with a GROUP BY becomes typically one MapReduce job: map reads the data and emits grouped keys, reduce aggregates. A query with multiple joins may become several chained MapReduce jobs. The output of one job feeds the input of the next. This translation is why HQL queries are slower than native MapReduce code — the abstraction adds stages that hand-written MapReduce could avoid.

GROUP: Hadoop, HDFS & Hive Architecture
Q: What is the difference between a Combiner and a Reducer?
A: A Reducer is guaranteed to receive all values for a given key from all mappers across the entire cluster — it produces the final correct output. A Combiner is a local optimization that may or may not run, may run multiple times, and receives only the output from one mapper's local data. Because the framework doesn't guarantee Combiner execution, it can only be used for operations that are idempotent and associative — SUM is fine, AVG is not, because (sum_A / count_A + sum_B / count_B) / 2 is not the same as (sum_A + sum_B) / (count_A + count_B). For average, the Combiner would need to emit (sum, count) pairs, and the Reducer computes the final average from the combined sums and counts. The Reducer always runs; the Combiner is a hint that may be skipped.

GROUP: Hadoop, HDFS & Hive Architecture
Q: What is the InputFormat in MapReduce?
A: InputFormat defines how the input data is split and read for map tasks. It has two responsibilities: creating input splits — determining how many mappers to launch and what data each gets — and providing a RecordReader that converts each split into key-value pairs the mapper processes. TextInputFormat is the default — treats each line as a value. SequenceFileInputFormat handles Hadoop's binary sequence files. OrcInputFormat reads ORC files and is used by Hive under the hood for ORC tables. CombineFileInputFormat merges many small files into larger splits, reducing the number of map tasks — useful for the small files problem. At HPCL, Hive abstracted InputFormat selection away from me for most cases — ORC tables automatically used OrcInputFormat, which is what enabled predicate pushdown and vectorized reads.

GROUP: Hadoop, HDFS & Hive Architecture
Q: Did you work with MapReduce directly or only through Hive HQL?
A: Almost entirely through HQL. Hive translates queries to MapReduce behind the scenes, so I wrote HQL and the framework handled the translation. The value of Hive is exactly that abstraction — what would require hundreds of lines of Java MapReduce code is expressed in a few lines of SQL. The cases where I went closer to MapReduce internals were tuning — adjusting the number of reducers, enabling Combiners, configuring memory settings for map and reduce tasks. But I never wrote raw MapReduce Java code in production. Today I'd be even further from MapReduce — Hive on Tez or Spark is the standard, where the execution engine isn't MapReduce at all. MapReduce knowledge is still relevant for understanding the underlying model, but the day-to-day interface is always a higher-level abstraction.

GROUP: Hadoop, HDFS & Hive Architecture
Q: What is the Map phase output format — key-value pairs — and how does the Reduce phase receive them?
A: The Map phase outputs key-value pairs where the key determines which reducer receives the pair. After the Map phase completes, the framework runs a Shuffle and Sort — it collects all mapper outputs, groups them by key, sorts them within each key, and routes them to the appropriate reducer based on the Partitioner. Each reducer receives its input as an iterator over sorted key-value pairs — all pairs with the same key arrive together, sorted. The Reducer processes each key's iterator in sequence. This sorted delivery is what makes aggregations efficient — the reducer doesn't need to build an in-memory hash table; it just iterates through already-grouped values. At HPCL when Hive generated MapReduce jobs for our demand aggregations, the sort-and-group behavior is what allowed efficient SUM and COUNT operations per plant without loading all records into memory simultaneously.

GROUP: Hadoop, HDFS & Hive Architecture
Q: What is YARN and what problem did it solve over Hadoop 1.x?
A: In Hadoop 1.x, MapReduce served two roles simultaneously — job scheduling and cluster resource management. This meant only MapReduce jobs could run on a Hadoop cluster, and the resource management was tightly coupled to the MapReduce execution model. YARN separated these two concerns. YARN handles resource management as a general-purpose cluster resource manager. Applications — MapReduce, Spark, Tez, Flink, or anything else — plug in as first-class citizens. At HPCL this mattered because we ran both Hive jobs (which used MapReduce, then migrated to Tez) and Spark jobs on the same cluster. Without YARN we'd need separate clusters for different execution engines. YARN let us share one physical cluster across all workloads with configurable resource allocation between them.

GROUP: Hadoop, HDFS & Hive Architecture
Q: Explain the YARN architecture components — ResourceManager, NodeManager, ApplicationMaster.
A: ResourceManager is the master — it manages cluster resources globally and decides which applications get how much. It has two main services: the Scheduler, which allocates resources, and the ApplicationsManager, which handles application lifecycle. NodeManager runs on every worker node — it monitors that node's CPU and memory, launches containers as instructed by the ResourceManager, and reports node health. ApplicationMaster is per-application — when you submit a Spark job or a Hive query, YARN launches an ApplicationMaster for that specific job. The ApplicationMaster negotiates resources from the ResourceManager, requests containers from NodeManagers, and manages the job's execution within those containers. At HPCL the Spark ApplicationMaster was the Spark driver, negotiating executor containers from YARN.

GROUP: Hadoop, HDFS & Hive Architecture
Q: What is a Container in YARN?
A: A Container is an allocation of CPU and memory resources on a specific worker node — it's the unit of resource allocation in YARN. When an ApplicationMaster needs to run a task, it requests a Container from the ResourceManager specifying required memory and CPU cores. The ResourceManager finds a NodeManager with sufficient available capacity and allocates the Container. The NodeManager then launches a process inside that Container — a mapper task, a Spark executor, a reduce task, or any other unit of work. At HPCL tuning Container memory settings was important for Hive workloads — too little memory per Container and tasks spill to disk; too much and we left cluster resources unused. The right balance was found by profiling actual memory usage per query type in the Spark UI or YARN Resource Manager UI.

GROUP: Hadoop, HDFS & Hive Architecture
Q: What is the difference between the YARN Scheduler and ApplicationsManager?
A: The YARN ResourceManager contains both. The Scheduler is responsible only for resource allocation — it decides which application gets which containers based on capacity rules or fairness policies. It doesn't start application containers or monitor them. The ApplicationsManager handles the lifecycle of running applications — accepting job submissions, negotiating the Container for the ApplicationMaster, restarting the ApplicationMaster on failure, and cleaning up after completion. The Scheduler is pluggable — you can swap it for the CapacityScheduler or FairScheduler without changing the ApplicationsManager. At HPCL we ran the CapacityScheduler with separate queues for production ETL jobs and ad-hoc analyst queries — production jobs always had guaranteed minimum capacity and could burst to more, while analyst queries ran in a separate queue that couldn't starve production.

GROUP: Hadoop, HDFS & Hive Architecture
Q: What is the Capacity Scheduler in YARN?
A: The Capacity Scheduler divides cluster resources into queues, each with a guaranteed minimum capacity and a maximum cap. Jobs submitted to a queue can use up to their queue's maximum capacity when the cluster has idle resources, but always get their minimum guaranteed capacity even when the cluster is busy. At HPCL I configured a production queue with 60% minimum capacity for the nightly ETL jobs that had strict 11 AM delivery SLAs, and an analytics queue with 30% minimum for ad-hoc analyst queries. The remaining 10% was a shared burst pool. This ensured that when an analyst ran a large exploratory query, it couldn't starve the production ETL that regional planners depended on for morning indent recommendations. Queue admission control also let us restrict which Hive users could submit to which queue.

GROUP: Hadoop, HDFS & Hive Architecture
Q: What is the Fair Scheduler in YARN and when would you use it?
A: The Fair Scheduler dynamically balances resources across all running applications — if only one application is running it gets all resources, and as more applications submit, resources are shared equally. Unlike the Capacity Scheduler's fixed queue-based guarantees, the Fair Scheduler allocates resources fluidly based on active demand. You'd use it in multi-tenant environments where fair sharing between all concurrent users is the priority rather than guaranteed capacity for specific queues. At HPCL we chose the Capacity Scheduler over Fair because production ETL had hard SLA requirements — we needed guaranteed minimum resources regardless of how many ad-hoc queries were running. Fair Scheduler would have risked production ETL being starved during peak analyst activity periods. For a research cluster where all users have equal priority, Fair Scheduler is the better default.

GROUP: Hadoop, HDFS & Hive Architecture
Q: How does YARN handle ApplicationMaster failure?
A: YARN monitors each ApplicationMaster through periodic heartbeats. If the ApplicationMaster stops heartbeating — due to process crash, node failure, or OOM kill — the ResourceManager detects the failure and can restart it, up to a configurable maximum number of attempts. When the ApplicationMaster restarts, it re-registers with the ResourceManager and can optionally recover in-progress tasks by re-requesting containers and reassigning work. For MapReduce and Hive on Tez, completed tasks don't need to re-run after ApplicationMaster recovery — only in-flight tasks are restarted. For Spark, the ApplicationMaster is the driver — if it fails and restarts, the entire job restarts from the beginning unless checkpointing was enabled. At HPCL we configured max-attempts=3 for production jobs and monitored YARN logs when retry alerts fired.

GROUP: Hadoop, HDFS & Hive Architecture
Q: Explain the Hive architecture end to end — Driver, Compiler, Metastore, Execution Engine.
A: A HQL query enters the Driver, which manages the query lifecycle. The Driver sends it to the Compiler. The Compiler parses the SQL into an abstract syntax tree, resolves table and column names against the Metastore to get schema information, and generates a logical query plan. The optimizer applies transformations — predicate pushdown, column pruning, join reordering based on table statistics. The optimized logical plan is converted to a physical plan — a DAG of MapReduce, Tez, or Spark stages depending on the configured execution engine. The physical plan is submitted to the Execution Engine — YARN for MapReduce or Tez, or Spark. Results flow back through the Driver to the client. The Metastore is the backbone throughout — without it the Compiler can't resolve anything. At HPCL the Metastore was the most carefully maintained component; a Metastore outage would bring down all Hive operations.

GROUP: Hadoop, HDFS & Hive Architecture
Q: What is the Hive Metastore and why is it important?
A: The Hive Metastore is a relational database — typically MySQL or PostgreSQL — that stores all Hive metadata: table definitions, column names, data types, partition information, storage locations on HDFS, SerDe properties, and table statistics. Without the Metastore, Hive can't do anything — the Compiler queries it to resolve table schemas for every query. If the Metastore goes down, all Hive queries fail immediately. At HPCL the Metastore was on a dedicated, highly available MySQL instance with replication — it was treated with the same production care as the HDFS NameNode. We backed it up nightly and practiced restore procedures. The Metastore is also why HDFS data can be shared across tools — Spark, Presto, and Hive can all query the same HDFS data because they all read the same Metastore for schema information.

GROUP: Hadoop, HDFS & Hive Architecture
Q: What is HiveServer2 and why use it instead of the Hive CLI?
A: HiveServer2 is a service that allows multiple clients to connect to Hive simultaneously over JDBC or ODBC connections. The Hive CLI is a single-user command-line interface that runs a local Hive session — it can't be accessed by remote clients and doesn't support concurrent connections. HiveServer2 enables: BI tools like Tableau or Power BI to connect via JDBC, multiple analysts to run queries simultaneously with proper session isolation, Kerberos authentication and fine-grained access control, and query monitoring via the built-in web UI. At HPCL, HiveServer2 was the production interface — analysts connected from their laptops via JDBC, and the BI platform connected via ODBC. The Hive CLI was only used by engineers for administrative and debugging tasks directly on the cluster.

GROUP: Hadoop, HDFS & Hive Architecture
Q: What is a SerDe in Hive and when do you need a custom one?
A: SerDe stands for Serializer/Deserializer — it defines how Hive reads data from files and writes data back. Every Hive table has a SerDe that translates between the raw file bytes and Hive's internal row representation. LazySimpleSerDe handles delimited text files. OrcSerde handles ORC files. JsonSerDe handles JSON. You need a custom SerDe when your data format isn't supported by any built-in SerDe — for example, a proprietary binary format, a non-standard delimiter, or a hierarchical structure that requires custom parsing logic. At HPCL our SCADA data landed as pipe-delimited text files with a custom header format that the standard SerDe couldn't parse correctly — we configured a custom field delimiter and skip-header settings rather than a full custom SerDe, but for genuinely unusual formats writing a custom Java SerDe and registering it in the Metastore is the right approach.

GROUP: Hadoop, HDFS & Hive Architecture
Q: What is the difference between Hive on MapReduce, Tez, and Spark?
A: All three are execution engines for Hive — they run the physical plan Hive generates. MapReduce was the original engine — it writes intermediate data to disk between every stage, making multi-stage queries very slow. Tez replaced MapReduce as the default for Hive — it uses an in-memory DAG execution model that pipelines stages without writing to disk between them. A query that required 5 MapReduce jobs chained through HDFS might run as one Tez DAG with the stages connected in memory. Spark as Hive's engine is similar to Tez — in-memory DAG execution — but uses Spark's execution model. At HPCL we migrated from MapReduce to Tez and saw typical query speedups of 3–5x on multi-stage analytical queries. The multi-stage demand aggregation queries improved the most because intermediate data no longer touched HDFS between stages.

GROUP: Hadoop, HDFS & Hive Architecture
Q: What is cost-based optimization in Hive?
A: Cost-based optimization (CBO) uses table and column statistics — row counts, value histograms, null counts, cardinality — to estimate the cost of different query plans and choose the cheapest one. Without CBO, Hive uses rule-based optimization only — applying fixed transformation rules without considering actual data sizes. With CBO enabled via hive.cbo.enable=true, Hive can make better join ordering decisions — putting the smaller table on the build side of a hash join — and better choice between join strategies. CBO requires up-to-date statistics: ANALYZE TABLE fills COMPUTE STATISTICS FOR COLUMNS plant_id, fill_date. At HPCL I ran statistics gathering after every significant data load. Without current statistics the optimizer routinely made bad join ordering decisions that caused queries to take 10x longer than necessary.

GROUP: Hadoop, HDFS & Hive Architecture
Q: What is predicate pushdown in Hive and how does it work?
A: Predicate pushdown moves filter conditions as close to the data source as possible — ideally into the file reader itself, before data is even loaded into memory. For ORC tables, the ORC reader can use column-level min/max statistics to skip entire row groups that can't possibly match the filter, and bloom filters to skip row groups that almost certainly don't match. For partitioned tables, partition pruning is a form of predicate pushdown — only relevant partitions are read. Enable with hive.optimize.ppd=true. At HPCL a query filtering on plant_id = 'KONDAPALLI' and fill_date >= '2025-01-01' with partition pruning and ORC predicate pushdown would skip 54 plants' partitions entirely and within the Kondapalli partition skip ORC row groups outside the date range — reading perhaps 1% of the total data volume.

GROUP: Hadoop, HDFS & Hive Architecture
Q: What is vectorized query execution in Hive?
A: Vectorized query execution processes a batch of rows — typically 1,024 at a time — using SIMD CPU instructions rather than processing one row at a time. The batch is held in columnar arrays in memory, and operations like filter, arithmetic, and aggregation are applied to the entire batch in tight CPU loops with no row-by-row function call overhead. Enable with hive.vectorized.execution.enabled=true on ORC tables. The performance gain on CPU-bound operations — aggregations, string comparisons, arithmetic — is typically 5–10x compared to row-at-a-time execution. At HPCL enabling vectorization on our ORC-formatted fill records table cut aggregation query time by about 4x on a demanding monthly variance analysis — the improvement was most visible on queries with complex WHERE clauses and GROUP BY across many rows.

GROUP: Hadoop, HDFS & Hive Architecture
Q: What is LLAP in Hive and what performance does it enable?
A: LLAP — Live Long and Process — is Hive's persistent daemon-based execution model introduced in Hive 2.x. Instead of launching fresh JVM processes for each query — which has significant startup overhead — LLAP keeps long-running daemon processes resident on cluster nodes with pre-warmed JVMs, cached data in off-heap memory, and persistent I/O threads. Queries execute inside these already-running daemons rather than spawning new containers per query. The result is sub-second to few-second latency for interactive analytical queries — Hive can behave more like Impala or Presto for interactive use. At HPCL we didn't run LLAP in production because our workload was primarily batch — nightly ETL and daily reports that weren't latency-sensitive. LLAP would be the right choice for an interactive analytics use case where analysts need query response times under 5 seconds.

GROUP: Hadoop, HDFS & Hive Architecture
Q: What is the Hive query execution lifecycle — from HQL string to result?
A: The lifecycle has six steps. One — parsing: the HQL string is parsed into an abstract syntax tree. Two — semantic analysis: table and column names are resolved against the Metastore, permissions are checked, and the AST becomes a query block tree. Three — logical plan generation: the query block tree becomes a logical operator DAG — TableScan, Filter, GroupBy, Join, Select. Four — logical optimization: rule-based transformations apply — predicate pushdown, column pruning, constant folding, join reordering. Five — physical plan generation: the logical DAG is converted to MapReduce, Tez, or Spark stages. Six — execution: the physical plan is submitted to the execution engine via YARN, tasks run, intermediate data is managed, and results are returned to the Driver and then the client. Table statistics from the Metastore influence steps three and four.

GROUP: Hadoop, HDFS & Hive Architecture
Q: What is the difference between a Hive managed table and an external table?
A: For a managed table, Hive owns both the metadata in the Metastore and the data files on HDFS — when you DROP the table, Hive deletes the data. For an external table, Hive owns only the metadata — the data files on HDFS are managed independently, and DROP TABLE removes only the Metastore entry while the HDFS data remains intact. At HPCL I used external tables exclusively for production data — the HDFS data files were owned by the ETL pipeline and needed to survive any Hive Metastore operations. If an analyst accidentally dropped an external table, the data was safe and I could recreate the table definition in minutes. Managed tables I only used for temporary staging tables within the ETL workflow where I actively wanted DROP TABLE to clean up both metadata and data together.

GROUP: Hadoop, HDFS & Hive Architecture
Q: When would you use a managed table versus an external table?
A: External tables when: data is shared between multiple tools — Spark, Hive, and Presto all reading the same HDFS files; data is produced by an external process you don't want Hive to control; or data must survive DROP TABLE operations. Managed tables when: the data's lifecycle is fully controlled by Hive; you want DROP TABLE to clean up both data and metadata atomically; or you're using ACID transactions which require managed tables in older Hive versions. At HPCL the decision was simple — all production analytical data used external tables because the ETL pipeline owned the HDFS files, and Hive was just one of several consumers. Internal staging tables within a single ETL script used managed tables because dropping the staging table at the end of the job cleaning up the data was the desired behavior.

GROUP: Hadoop, HDFS & Hive Architecture
Q: How do you create an external table in Hive?
A: CREATE EXTERNAL TABLE cylinder_fills (fill_id BIGINT, plant_id STRING, fill_timestamp TIMESTAMP, fill_volume DOUBLE, equipment_id STRING) PARTITIONED BY (fill_date STRING, plant_id_part STRING) STORED AS ORC LOCATION '/user/hpcl/fills/'. The EXTERNAL keyword tells Hive the data is managed outside Hive. LOCATION specifies where on HDFS the data lives. PARTITIONED BY lists the partition columns. STORED AS ORC specifies the file format — which determines the SerDe. After creating the table you need to either ALTER TABLE ADD PARTITION for each existing partition or run MSCK REPAIR TABLE to auto-discover all partitions from the HDFS directory structure. The table definition lives in the Metastore; the data itself stays wherever the LOCATION points.

GROUP: Hadoop, HDFS & Hive Architecture
Q: How do you add a partition to a Hive table manually?
A: ALTER TABLE cylinder_fills ADD PARTITION (fill_date='2026-04-29', plant_id_part='KONDAPALLI') LOCATION '/user/hpcl/fills/fill_date=2026-04-29/plant_id=KONDAPALLI/'. This registers the partition in the Metastore so Hive knows to include it in queries. If you're adding many partitions at once, MSCK REPAIR TABLE is more efficient since it scans the HDFS directory structure and adds all unregistered partitions in one operation. The explicit ALTER TABLE ADD PARTITION is faster when you know the exact partition being added — it makes a targeted Metastore update rather than a full directory scan. At HPCL the daily ETL used ALTER TABLE ADD PARTITION for the specific date partition it created each night, reserving MSCK REPAIR TABLE for recovery scenarios when partitions were added via bulk HDFS operations.

GROUP: Hadoop, HDFS & Hive Architecture
Q: What is dynamic partition insertion and what are its performance risks?
A: Dynamic partitioning lets Hive read the partition column value from each row's data and route it to the correct partition automatically during an INSERT. You set hive.exec.dynamic.partition=true and hive.exec.dynamic.partition.mode=nonstrict. The risk is partition explosion — if the partition column has high cardinality, a single INSERT can try to create thousands of partitions simultaneously, each requiring a separate HDFS directory and Metastore entry. Thousands of concurrent Metastore writes can lock up the database underneath and fail the job. At HPCL I bounded dynamic partitioning carefully — the partition key was fill_date at month granularity, giving at most 12 new partitions per year. Daily partitioning would have been safe for our workload but I avoided anything with cardinality in the thousands. I also set hive.exec.max.dynamic.partitions=500 as a safety limit.

GROUP: Hadoop, HDFS & Hive Architecture
Q: What is partitioning in Hive and what problem does it solve?
A: Partitioning organizes Hive table data into separate HDFS subdirectories based on column values — typically date or a categorical field like plant_id. When a query filters on the partition column, Hive's partition pruning reads only the relevant subdirectories rather than the entire table. This transforms what would be a full table scan into a partial scan proportional to the partition fraction being queried. At HPCL the cylinder fills table held 12 years of data — without partitioning, every query would scan the entire dataset. Partitioning by fill_date meant a query for one month read only 1/144th of the total data. The performance difference was dramatic — queries that took 45 minutes on unpartitioned data ran in under 3 minutes on the partitioned table with the same underlying data volume.

GROUP: Hadoop, HDFS & Hive Architecture
Q: What columns did you choose to partition on at HPCL and why?
A: I partitioned by fill_date at day granularity as the primary partition key, with plant_id as a secondary partition. Fill_date because nearly every analytical query filters by date range — daily reports, monthly aggregations, year-over-year comparisons all use date as a filter. Plant_id as secondary because plant-specific queries are the next most common access pattern — a plant manager querying their own data, or a regional report covering 8–10 specific plants. With this scheme, a query for Plant 12 in January 2026 reads only the fill_date=2026-01-*/plant_id=PLANT12/ subdirectories — a tiny fraction of the total data. Equipment type I considered as a tertiary partition but rejected it — equipment type queries were rarer and adding it would have caused partition proliferation without proportional benefit.

GROUP: Hadoop, HDFS & Hive Architecture
Q: What is partition pruning and how does Hive implement it?
A: Partition pruning is Hive's mechanism for skipping partitions that can't possibly contain rows matching a query's filter conditions. It works at the Metastore level — when the optimizer sees a WHERE clause on the partition column, it queries the Metastore for the list of partitions and eliminates those that don't match before a single HDFS file is read. For fill_date >= '2026-01-01' AND fill_date < '2026-04-01', Hive identifies all date partitions in that range from the Metastore and only reads those HDFS directories. Files in other partitions are never opened. The optimizer can also perform dynamic partition pruning — deferring which partitions to read until runtime based on a join's intermediate result. At HPCL, correctly structured partition predicates were the single most impactful query optimization available.

GROUP: Hadoop, HDFS & Hive Architecture
Q: What is bucketing in Hive and how is it different from partitioning?
A: Partitioning is coarse-grained — it creates directory-level separation based on a column's values, allowing entire directories to be skipped. Bucketing is fine-grained — within a partition it divides data into a fixed number of files based on a hash of a column. CLUSTERED BY equipment_id INTO 32 BUCKETS puts all records with equipment_id hash % 32 = 0 into bucket 0, hash % 32 = 1 into bucket 1, and so on. The main benefit of bucketing is join performance — if both tables are bucketed by the same column into the same number of buckets, Hive can do a bucket-to-bucket join without shuffling all data. At HPCL I used bucketing on the equipment_id column for the predictive maintenance analytics table — joins between the fill records and maintenance events on equipment_id became much faster because matching bucket files were joined without a full shuffle.

GROUP: Hadoop, HDFS & Hive Architecture
Q: How do you create a bucketed table in Hive?
A: CREATE TABLE maintenance_events (event_id BIGINT, equipment_id STRING, event_date DATE, maintenance_type STRING, labor_hours DOUBLE) PARTITIONED BY (event_year INT) CLUSTERED BY (equipment_id) INTO 32 BUCKETS STORED AS ORC. The number of buckets should be a power of 2 for efficient bucket-to-bucket joins. To actually populate buckets correctly you need SET hive.enforce.bucketing=true before inserting — without this, Hive may write data without respecting bucket boundaries even if the table definition specifies bucketing. Also SET hive.exec.reducers.bytes.per.reducer to a value that produces the right number of reducers, since each reducer writes one bucket file. At HPCL I verified bucketing was working correctly by checking that the number of output files equaled the number of buckets after each insert.

GROUP: Hadoop, HDFS & Hive Architecture
Q: What is a sorted bucket table and when do you use it?
A: A sorted bucket table is bucketed plus sorted within each bucket by a specified column — CLUSTERED BY (equipment_id) SORTED BY (event_date ASC) INTO 32 BUCKETS. The sort is applied within each bucket file. When two sorted bucket tables are joined on the bucket column, Hive can use a sort-merge join that simply merges two already-sorted streams rather than building a hash table — more memory-efficient and faster for large tables. The tradeoff is that inserts must maintain sort order, adding overhead to writes. At HPCL I used sorted bucketing on the maintenance events table when the primary analytical pattern was equipment_id-keyed joins with the fill records — the sort-merge join on sorted buckets was significantly faster than hash join alternatives for that specific many-to-many join across 12 years of maintenance history.

GROUP: Hadoop, HDFS & Hive Architecture
Q: What are the disadvantages of over-partitioning?
A: Over-partitioning creates too many small HDFS directories and files, which reintroduces the small files problem at the partition level. If I partition by fill_date at day granularity and equipment_id — 365 days × potentially hundreds of equipment IDs — I could have tens of thousands of partitions, each with a tiny file. Each partition requires a Metastore entry, so the Metastore database grows dramatically and queries that need to enumerate partitions — like SELECT COUNT(*) without a date filter — become slow just from the Metastore metadata query. Also, very small partitions are slower to read than fewer larger files because task scheduling overhead dominates. At HPCL I avoided adding equipment_type as a tertiary partition for this reason — the 55 plants × 365 days partition structure was already on the edge of comfortable Metastore size.

GROUP: Hadoop, HDFS & Hive Architecture
Q: How did you decide between partitioning by date vs by month vs by year?
A: The right granularity matches the most common query filter granularity — coarser than queries need forces reading more data than necessary, finer than queries need creates too many small partitions. At HPCL analysts always queried specific date ranges — most commonly day-level for operational reports and month-level for management summaries. Partitioning by day (fill_date = '2026-04-29') supported both: day-level queries hit one partition, month-level queries hit 28–31 partitions. Partitioning by month would have forced day-level queries to read an entire month. Partitioning by year would have forced monthly queries to read a full year. Daily partitioning produced 55 plants × 365 days = ~20,000 partitions per year — manageable but required monitoring Metastore growth. For a table with only yearly queries, year-level partitioning would be appropriate.

GROUP: Hadoop, HDFS & Hive Architecture
Q: What is the difference between partition and bucket in terms of query performance benefit?
A: Partitioning benefits queries that filter on the partition column — it eliminates irrelevant data at the directory level before reading any files. The benefit is dramatic for queries that scan a small fraction of partitions. Bucketing benefits join performance — when joining two tables bucketed on the same column with the same number of buckets, matching buckets are joined together without full shuffles. Bucketing also enables efficient sampling — SELECT * FROM table TABLESAMPLE(BUCKET 1 OUT OF 32) reads only one bucket. Partitioning is about filtering; bucketing is about joining and sampling. They're complementary — at HPCL I used partitioning on date and plant_id for the filter benefit on date-range queries, and bucketing on equipment_id within the maintenance table for join performance with the equipment master.

GROUP: Hadoop, HDFS & Hive Architecture
Q: What HQL window functions did you use and give a real HPCL example?
A: The three I used most frequently were ROW_NUMBER for deduplication and latest-record queries, LAG for day-over-day trend analysis, and running SUM for cumulative tracking. A concrete example from the demand forecasting work: SELECT plant_id, fill_date, daily_volume, SUM(daily_volume) OVER (PARTITION BY plant_id ORDER BY fill_date ROWS BETWEEN 6 PRECEDING AND CURRENT ROW) AS rolling_7day_sum, daily_volume - LAG(daily_volume, 1, 0) OVER (PARTITION BY plant_id ORDER BY fill_date) AS day_over_day_change FROM daily_plant_fills. The rolling 7-day sum fed the moving average feature used in the Prophet forecasting model. The day-over-day change was a direct feature in the XGBoost churn model — distributors showing consistent negative daily volume change were flagged as early churn signals.

GROUP: Hadoop, HDFS & Hive Architecture
Q: How do you write a CTE in HQL and why use it over a subquery?
A: WITH staging AS (SELECT plant_id, fill_date, SUM(fill_volume) AS total_volume FROM cylinder_fills WHERE fill_date >= '2026-01-01' GROUP BY plant_id, fill_date), plant_stats AS (SELECT plant_id, AVG(total_volume) AS avg_daily_volume, STDDEV(total_volume) AS std_daily_volume FROM staging GROUP BY plant_id) SELECT s.plant_id, s.fill_date, s.total_volume, p.avg_daily_volume, (s.total_volume - p.avg_daily_volume) / p.std_daily_volume AS z_score FROM staging s JOIN plant_stats p ON s.plant_id = p.plant_id. Compared to nested subqueries, CTEs make the query read top-to-bottom as a logical sequence. Each named block is independently readable. Debugging is easier — I can run just the first CTE and inspect results without executing the full query.

GROUP: Hadoop, HDFS & Hive Architecture
Q: How do you optimize a slow Hive query — 5-step approach?
A: Step one: check whether partition pruning is active — verify the WHERE clause uses the partition column exactly and isn't wrapped in a function. Step two: check file format — if the table is text format, convert to ORC. ORC alone often delivers 5x improvement. Step three: check table statistics — run ANALYZE TABLE and verify EXPLAIN shows accurate row estimates. Stale statistics cause bad join ordering. Step four: check for map-side join opportunities — if one join table is small, enable hive.auto.convert.join and verify the plan shows a MapJoin. Step five: check reducer count — too few reducers means each handles too much data, too many creates scheduling overhead. At HPCL this sequence resolved 90% of slow query reports without needing any structural changes to the queries themselves.

GROUP: Hadoop, HDFS & Hive Architecture
Q: What is hive.exec.parallel and when do you enable it?
A: hive.exec.parallel allows independent stages of a Hive query — those with no data dependency on each other — to run simultaneously rather than sequentially. In a query with two independent subqueries that are later joined, without parallel execution they run one at a time. With it enabled they run concurrently, cutting total wall-clock time by roughly half for two equally expensive independent stages. Enable with SET hive.exec.parallel=true and SET hive.exec.parallel.thread.number=8 to control how many parallel stages run. The tradeoff: parallel stages compete for cluster resources. At HPCL I enabled it selectively for the complex multi-stage demand forecast pipeline where several regional aggregations were independent and could safely run simultaneously — it cut that pipeline's runtime from 45 minutes to about 28 minutes.

GROUP: Hadoop, HDFS & Hive Architecture
Q: What are Hive built-in aggregate functions and which did you use most at HPCL?
A: The standard ones: COUNT, SUM, AVG, MIN, MAX, STDDEV, VARIANCE, COLLECT_SET (unique values as an array), COLLECT_LIST (all values as an array), PERCENTILE_APPROX for approximate percentiles on large datasets, and CORR for correlation. At HPCL I used SUM and COUNT daily for operational aggregations. STDDEV and AVG together for deviation detection — computing how far each plant's fill volume was from its mean as a quality signal. PERCENTILE_APPROX for the distributor performance scoring — computing the 75th and 90th percentile of performance scores network-wide for tier boundary setting. COLLECT_SET for audit analytics — collecting distinct complaint categories per distributor into an array for downstream processing. CORR was my tool of choice in the $7.7M investigation to test pairwise correlations between operational variables and terminal loss.

GROUP: Hadoop, HDFS & Hive Architecture
Q: How do you handle NULL values in HQL queries — NVL, COALESCE, CASE WHEN?
A: HQL supports COALESCE natively — COALESCE(complaint_count, 0) returns 0 when complaint_count is NULL. NVL is also available in most Hive versions as a synonym. For conditional logic, CASE WHEN complaint_count IS NULL THEN 0 ELSE complaint_count END handles it explicitly. For joins, NULL values in join keys cause those rows to not match — the same as Oracle. I use COALESCE by default because it's ANSI standard and works consistently across HQL, Spark SQL, and Oracle without dialect differences. At HPCL, NULL handling was most critical in the performance scoring pipeline — distributors with no complaint data needed to be scored as zero complaints, not excluded from scoring. Every aggregate feeding into the 100-point model was wrapped in COALESCE before aggregation to prevent NULL propagation from silently distorting the composite score.

GROUP: Hadoop, HDFS & Hive Architecture
Q: How do you write complex nested HQL — multiple subqueries vs CTEs vs views?
A: My preference hierarchy is CTEs first, views second, subqueries last. CTEs keep the query self-contained and readable — everything the query needs is defined at the top. Views encapsulate reusable logic that multiple queries need, but add a Metastore dependency that can surprise people when they try to understand a query. Nested subqueries more than two levels deep become nearly impossible to read or debug — at that point CTEs are always better. At HPCL the demand classification query had four logical steps — pulling raw fills, computing ADI and CV², classifying each plant's pattern, then joining back to plant master for the output. As four nested subqueries it was 80 lines of parenthesis-laden SQL. As four CTEs with named blocks it was the same logic, readable top to bottom, and debuggable by running to any intermediate step.

GROUP: Hadoop, HDFS & Hive Architecture
Q: How do you perform a self-join in HQL?
A: The syntax is identical to SQL — alias the table twice and join on the condition relating the two instances. SELECT a.equipment_id, a.event_date AS current_service, b.event_date AS previous_service, DATEDIFF(a.event_date, b.event_date) AS days_between FROM maintenance_events a JOIN maintenance_events b ON a.equipment_id = b.equipment_id AND b.event_date = (SELECT MAX(c.event_date) FROM maintenance_events c WHERE c.equipment_id = a.equipment_id AND c.event_date < a.event_date). At HPCL I used this pattern for computing time-between-failures per equipment — it was later rewritten using LAG() which is cleaner, but the self-join is the right approach when the query needs more than just adjacent rows. HQL handles self-joins correctly as long as both aliases are used explicitly and the join conditions are precise.

GROUP: Hadoop, HDFS & Hive Architecture
Q: What is ORC format and why is it preferred for Hive over text?
A: ORC — Optimized Row Columnar — stores data in a columnar format with several query performance features. Columnar storage means reading only the columns a query needs — a SELECT on 5 of 100 columns reads only 5% of the physical data. ORC includes column-level min/max statistics and bloom filters at the row group level, enabling predicate pushdown that skips row groups guaranteed not to match a filter. ORC supports lightweight compression per column — integer columns compress dramatically since values are often similar. ORC files self-describe their schema. And ORC supports vectorized reads — batch operations on columnar data. At HPCL switching the cylinder fills table from pipe-delimited text to ORC reduced storage by 65% and typical query time by 5–8x — purely from the format change, without any query or schema modifications.

GROUP: Hadoop, HDFS & Hive Architecture
Q: What is Parquet format and how does it differ from ORC?
A: Both are columnar formats with compression and predicate pushdown, but they differ in implementation details and ecosystem fit. ORC is optimized for Hive and Hadoop — it's the native format for Hive ACID, and Hive's CBO works best with ORC's richer column statistics. Parquet is the native format for the broader analytics ecosystem — Spark, Presto, Pandas, Arrow all read Parquet natively. ORC's row groups — called stripes — are typically larger (64–256 MB). Parquet's row groups are typically smaller (128 MB default). Parquet stores nested data more naturally due to its Dremel-derived encoding. At HPCL I used ORC for all Hive production tables because Hive's optimizations — vectorization, predicate pushdown, ACID — work best with ORC. For data shared with Spark or written by Spark jobs, I used Parquet to avoid format conversion overhead.

GROUP: Hadoop, HDFS & Hive Architecture
Q: What is the difference between row-oriented and columnar storage?
A: Row-oriented storage — CSV, text, most OLTP databases — stores all columns of a row together on disk. Reading any column requires reading every column of each row. Columnar storage — ORC, Parquet — stores each column's values together. Reading only selected columns is efficient because only those columns' data is read from disk. For analytical queries that select a few columns from wide tables, columnar is dramatically more efficient. Columnar also compresses better — a column of integer plant_ids with 55 distinct values compresses to near nothing with run-length encoding. Row-oriented compresses less because adjacent bytes in a row are different types and semantically unrelated. The tradeoff: row-oriented is faster for reading or writing a complete row — better for transactional workloads. At HPCL with 100+ column tables and queries rarely touching more than 10, columnar storage was the obvious choice.

GROUP: Hadoop, HDFS & Hive Architecture
Q: What is compression in ORC — which codecs and what are the tradeoffs?
A: ORC supports three compression codecs: NONE, ZLIB, and SNAPPY. ZLIB — also called deflate — gives the highest compression ratio, often 3–5x for typical operational data. The tradeoff is higher CPU cost for compression and decompression. SNAPPY compresses less aggressively — typically 1.5–2.5x — but is much faster to decompress because it prioritizes speed over ratio. NONE is useful when data is already compressed or when I/O is not the bottleneck. At HPCL I used SNAPPY for production ORC tables. The cylinder fill data read frequently by Hive jobs benefited more from fast decompression than maximum compression ratio — SNAPPY reduced I/O substantially while adding minimal CPU overhead during reads. For archival data read infrequently, ZLIB would be preferable since the compression ratio savings in storage cost outweigh the CPU overhead.

GROUP: Hadoop, HDFS & Hive Architecture
Q: What is an ORC stripe and what are its components?
A: An ORC file is divided into stripes — large horizontal chunks of rows, typically 64–256 MB each. Each stripe contains three sections: the index data, the row data, and a stripe footer. The index data holds column-level statistics — min, max, sum, count — for each group of 10,000 rows within the stripe (row groups). The row data holds the actual column values in columnar format with encoding and compression applied. The stripe footer describes the column encodings and the positions of each stream within the stripe. At the file level there's also a file footer listing all stripes and their statistics, and a postscript with file metadata. At HPCL the stripe-level statistics were what enabled predicate pushdown — when querying fill_volume > 15.0, ORC's index data could identify which stripes had max fill_volume below 15.0 and skip them entirely.

GROUP: Hadoop, HDFS & Hive Architecture
Q: What is a bloom filter in ORC and how does it enable predicate pushdown?
A: A bloom filter is a probabilistic data structure that answers "is this value definitely not in this set?" with zero false negatives — if it says no, the value definitely isn't there. ORC can store bloom filters per column per row group. When a query filters on a specific value — WHERE equipment_id = 'EQ-2103' — the ORC reader checks the bloom filter for each row group. If the bloom filter says the value is not in that row group, the row group is skipped without reading its data. False positives are possible — the filter might say a value could be present when it isn't — but false negatives are impossible, so skipping is always safe. At HPCL I enabled bloom filters on equipment_id and plant_id columns in our ORC tables because point lookups on those columns were common in the maintenance analytics queries and bloom filters cut read volume significantly.

GROUP: Hadoop, HDFS & Hive Architecture
Q: What is schema evolution in Hive and how do you handle it safely?
A: Schema evolution is when a table's column structure needs to change over time — adding columns, renaming them, or changing types. The safest approach is additive-only evolution: only add new columns, never drop or rename existing ones. Adding a column with ALTER TABLE fills ADD COLUMNS (new_col STRING) is backward-compatible — existing data files that don't have the new column return NULL for it when queried. ORC format handles this gracefully since column references are by name in the schema, not by position. At HPCL I enforced an additive-only schema evolution policy for all production Hive tables. When a column needed to be logically renamed, I added the new column and populated it via an ETL step, deprecated the old column in the data dictionary, and only dropped it after all downstream consumers were updated — usually spanning one or two quarters.

GROUP: Hadoop, HDFS & Hive Architecture
Q: What is the ALTER TABLE command and what can you do with it in Hive?
A: ALTER TABLE supports several categories of changes. Schema changes: ADD COLUMNS to append new columns, CHANGE COLUMN to rename or retype a column (with caveats around backward compatibility), REPLACE COLUMNS to redefine the entire column list. Partition management: ADD PARTITION, DROP PARTITION, RENAME PARTITION. Table property updates: SET TBLPROPERTIES to change table metadata. SerDe changes: SET SERDE to switch the serialization format. Location changes: SET LOCATION to point the table to a different HDFS path. Format migration: COMPACT and CONCATENATE for ORC file management. At HPCL I used ALTER TABLE most commonly for ADD PARTITION (registering new daily partitions), ADD COLUMNS (schema evolution), and SET TBLPROPERTIES for updating data dictionary metadata like the last refresh timestamp and data owner contact.

GROUP: Hadoop, HDFS & Hive Architecture
Q: How do you migrate a Hive table from text format to ORC without data loss?
A: The safe pattern is create-then-swap. First, create a new ORC table with the same schema: CREATE TABLE fills_orc STORED AS ORC AS SELECT * FROM fills_text. This populates the new ORC table from the text source. Verify row counts and checksums match between the two tables. Then swap the external table pointer: if it's an external table, recreate the table definition pointing to the ORC files, or rename: ALTER TABLE fills_text RENAME TO fills_text_backup; ALTER TABLE fills_orc RENAME TO fills. At HPCL I ran the migration during a maintenance window and validated using COUNT(*) and SUM of key numeric columns before and after. Only after two weeks of parallel running — reading from the ORC table while keeping the text backup — did I DROP TABLE fills_text_backup and reclaim the storage.

GROUP: Hadoop, HDFS & Hive Architecture
Q: What are table statistics in Hive and why do they matter for query planning?
A: Table statistics include row count, total data size, and per-column statistics like number of distinct values, min, max, null count, and value histograms. The Hive optimizer uses these when cost-based optimization is enabled to estimate the cardinality of intermediate query results and choose the optimal join ordering and join strategy. Without accurate statistics, the optimizer guesses — and wrong guesses cause bad plans. A join where the optimizer thinks the larger table is small might not get a map-side join optimization it would have received with correct stats. At HPCL after every significant data load — monthly data imports, partition additions — I ran ANALYZE TABLE fills COMPUTE STATISTICS FOR COLUMNS plant_id, fill_date, fill_volume. Keeping statistics current was a documented part of the ETL runbook, not an afterthought.

GROUP: Hadoop, HDFS & Hive Architecture
Q: How do you back up and restore Hive Metastore?
A: The Metastore is a relational database — MySQL or PostgreSQL typically — so backup and restore use standard database tools. For MySQL: mysqldump -u hive -p hive_metastore > metastore_backup_$(date +%Y%m%d).sql. Restore: mysql -u hive -p hive_metastore < metastore_backup_20260429.sql. I schedule nightly mysqldump via cron and store backups in both local storage and HDFS. At HPCL we practiced Metastore restore quarterly — an untested backup is worth nothing. The restore test involved spinning up a separate Hive instance pointing to the restored Metastore and verifying table definitions, partition counts, and sample queries matched the production Metastore. HDFS data is the source of truth; the Metastore is reconstructable from HDFS using MSCK REPAIR TABLE if needed, but maintaining a current Metastore backup is orders of magnitude faster than rebuilding from scratch.

GROUP: Hadoop, HDFS & Hive Architecture
Q: How did you implement data lineage tracking at HPCL — from SCADA to BI?
A: I maintained a lineage document structured as a field-level chain — for each field in the BI dashboards, the document traced: source system (SCADA PLC controller ID), landing in HDFS (table, partition, field name), transformation in HQL (the specific query and transformation logic applied), aggregation in Power BI (measure or column definition), and the business owner and update frequency. The document lived in a shared repository alongside the ETL scripts. Any ETL change required updating the lineage doc as part of the same pull request — no code change was complete without lineage documentation being current. At HPCL this let us trace a discrepant dashboard number back to the exact SCADA sensor and transformation within hours, which was critical during the $7.7M supplier audit when we had to demonstrate data integrity under adversarial scrutiny.

GROUP: Hadoop, HDFS & Hive Architecture
Q: What is a data dictionary and how did you maintain one at HPCL?
A: The data dictionary was a structured catalog of every field in the analytics data layer — stored in a shared spreadsheet versioned in our repository. For each field I documented: field name, table name, data type, source system, source field name, transformation rule applied, business definition, units, valid value range, data owner, and any known data quality issues. Example entry: field = fill_volume_kg, table = cylinder_fills, type = DOUBLE, source = SCADA_PLC controller reading, transformation = raw_reading × calibration_factor, definition = net fill weight per cylinder in kilograms, range = 12.5–15.0, owner = Operations Analytics, quality note = calibration factor updated annually. Reviews happened quarterly and whenever a source system changed. Without this, institutional knowledge lived only in the heads of the people who built the pipelines — a serious operational risk.

GROUP: Hadoop, HDFS & Hive Architecture
Q: What is data traceability and why is it critical in a banking context?
A: Data traceability is the ability to follow data from its origin through every transformation to its final use — knowing where every number in a report came from and how it was computed. In a banking context this is a regulatory requirement, not just good practice. BCBS 239 requires banks to be able to aggregate risk data accurately and quickly, with full audit trails. Regulatory reports like CCAR and DFAST require demonstrating that each input figure can be traced back to source systems with documented transformations. If an examiner questions a number, the bank must produce the full provenance chain on short notice. At HPCL traceability mattered for the supplier audit — we had to demonstrate that the terminal loss data we used in the $7.7M analysis came from authenticated SCADA sources with no manipulation between source and analysis.

GROUP: Hadoop, HDFS & Hive Architecture
Q: How would you resolve a metadata or data sourcing discrepancy in production?
A: My approach: start at the report and work backward. Step one — identify the specific metric showing the discrepancy and the date range. Step two — check the Hive query or Power BI measure that computes it — sometimes the issue is a DAX or HQL logic error. Step three — check the underlying Hive table row counts against what the source ETL should have loaded — missing partitions or double loads are common. Step four — trace back to the ETL log for that date range — look for failures, retries, or partial completions. Step five — compare the source system data against the landed HDFS data using a checksum or row count reconciliation. At HPCL I found a SCADA retransmission bug this way — dashboard totals were double-counting because the same fill event appeared twice in HDFS with different received timestamps but identical physical timestamps.

GROUP: Hadoop, HDFS & Hive Architecture
Q: What tools exist for automated data lineage tracking?
A: Apache Atlas is the most common open-source option in the Hadoop ecosystem — it integrates with Hive, Spark, and HDFS to automatically capture and visualize lineage graphs. It hooks into Hive's hook mechanism and records every query, what tables it read, what tables it wrote, and what transformations it applied. OpenMetadata and Amundsen are newer catalog platforms that include lineage. In the cloud ecosystem, AWS Glue DataBrew and Azure Purview provide managed lineage tracking. At HPCL we used manual documentation rather than automated tooling — the team size and pipeline complexity made a spreadsheet-based data dictionary practical. For a larger organization or a more complex pipeline graph with hundreds of tables and transformations, Apache Atlas or a commercial catalog like Alation would be the right investment.

GROUP: Hadoop, HDFS & Hive Architecture
Q: What is a data catalog and how does it differ from a data dictionary?
A: A data dictionary focuses on technical field-level definitions — column names, types, transformations, source systems. It's typically a document or spreadsheet. A data catalog is broader — it's an organized, searchable inventory of all data assets in an organization, including tables, reports, dashboards, pipelines, and models. It typically includes business context, ownership, usage metrics, data quality scores, lineage, and access controls — and is usually implemented as a software platform with search and browsing capabilities. The data dictionary is a component of what a data catalog contains. At HPCL we had a robust data dictionary but no formal data catalog — analysts had to ask the data engineering team to find relevant datasets. A catalog like Alation or Collibra would let analysts self-serve dataset discovery, which would have reduced the support burden on my team significantly.

GROUP: Hadoop, HDFS & Hive Architecture
Q: How did you design idempotent ETL pipelines on Hive at HPCL?
A: Idempotence means running the pipeline multiple times produces the same result as running it once — critical for safe retries after failures. The main mechanism in Hive is INSERT OVERWRITE for partition-level writes — each run rewrites the partition cleanly, so a rerun after failure doesn't create duplicate records. The pipeline also has explicit idempotency checks: before writing, verify the source data is complete (row count within expected range), deduplicate at the landing stage using ROW_NUMBER, and write to a staging partition first before overwriting the production partition. If any step fails, the production partition is untouched because the write to staging failed before the final overwrite. At HPCL the daily ETL ran this way for 12 years without any instance of corrupted data reaching downstream reports due to partial pipeline completion.

GROUP: Hadoop, HDFS & Hive Architecture
Q: What is a staging table pattern in Hive ETL?
A: The staging table pattern lands raw data from the source system into a temporary Hive table first, applies data quality checks and transformations, then writes to the production table only after validation passes. The staging table holds the raw, possibly dirty data and is overwritten each run. At HPCL: step one, LOAD DATA from HDFS into fills_staging — raw SAP extracts with all original columns. Step two, run quality checks: row count within expected range, no null plant_ids, fill volumes within physical bounds. Step three, if quality passes, INSERT OVERWRITE into fills_production with deduplication and transformations applied. Step four, if quality fails, alert and halt — fills_production retains yesterday's clean data. This pattern made it safe to rerun the pipeline after a source system anomaly without risking production data integrity.

GROUP: Hadoop, HDFS & Hive Architecture
Q: How did you schedule and monitor Hive ETL pipelines at HPCL?
A: Scheduling was via cron jobs — the daily ETL triggered at 10 PM after plant day-end completion events were confirmed. Shell scripts wrapped the HQL submissions with error handling, log capture, and alerting. Monitoring used three layers: job-level via YARN Resource Manager — job status, duration, resource usage. Output-level via reconciliation queries — row counts, date range coverage, and checksum comparisons run immediately after each pipeline stage. SLA monitoring via a status table updated by the pipeline itself — a Power BI dashboard tracked pipeline completion timestamps and flagged any job that hadn't completed by its SLA. At HPCL we needed results delivered by 11 AM, so any job still running at 10 AM triggered an automated alert to the operations team. We had zero SLA misses in the last 3 years of the pipeline's operation.

GROUP: Hadoop, HDFS & Hive Architecture
Q: How do you load data into Hive — LOAD DATA vs INSERT SELECT vs Sqoop?
A: Three main approaches. LOAD DATA moves or copies a file already on HDFS (or from local disk) into the Hive table's directory — it's fast because it just moves files, no processing. Good for raw ingestion when the source file is already in the right format. INSERT INTO / INSERT OVERWRITE runs a SELECT query and writes the result into the table — the flexible approach for transformations, deduplication, and format conversion. Sqoop imports data from relational databases — it parallelizes JDBC reads and writes directly to HDFS in your chosen format. At HPCL: Sqoop for SAP Oracle extraction to HDFS, then INSERT OVERWRITE into ORC Hive tables with transformation logic applied. LOAD DATA I used occasionally for bulk historical loads where the files were already in good shape. Never toPandas in the middle — the Hive layer always received data from HDFS directly.

GROUP: Hadoop, HDFS & Hive Architecture
Q: How did you handle late-arriving data in your Hive pipelines?
A: Late-arriving data — records from a prior date arriving in a later batch — is a common problem in operational data pipelines. At HPCL SCADA data occasionally arrived a day late due to network issues at remote plants. My approach: rather than only writing to today's date partition, the ETL checked each record's fill_timestamp and used INSERT OVERWRITE on the correct partition regardless of when the record arrived. For the affected partition, I ran the full deduplication and rewrite rather than appending. The downstream aggregation tables that consumed the fill records were also recomputed for any affected date partitions. The 11 AM delivery SLA applied to the current day's data — late-arriving data from prior days triggered a reprocessing run that completed outside the SLA window and was communicated separately to regional managers.

GROUP: Hadoop, HDFS & Hive Architecture
Q: When would you use Hive versus Spark SQL for the same analytical workload?
A: Hive when: the workload is scheduled batch that doesn't need interactive latency, the team is more comfortable with HQL, existing table definitions and partitioning strategies are already in Hive, or ACID transactions are needed. Spark SQL when: the workload needs lower latency, multiple iterations of transformations are needed (Spark's in-memory model wins here), the pipeline involves ML model training alongside the analytics, or data comes from diverse sources beyond Hive — Kafka, S3 Parquet, Delta Lake. At HPCL I used Hive for the core demand aggregation pipeline that ran on a fixed schedule — the HQL was readable by the operations analysts who needed to understand and occasionally modify it. I used Spark for the ML feature engineering steps that required iterative transformations and needed to feed directly into scikit-learn and Prophet model training.

GROUP: Hadoop, HDFS & Hive Architecture
Q: What is the difference between Hive and Impala?
A: Both query HDFS data using SQL, but they use different execution models. Hive is batch-oriented — queries run via MapReduce or Tez, with significant startup latency per query. It handles very long-running queries well. Impala is an MPP query engine — it uses massively parallel processing with persistent daemons on each node, no MapReduce startup overhead, and achieves sub-second to few-second latency for interactive queries. Impala trades off: it doesn't support all Hive features — some complex queries aren't supported, and it's more sensitive to data being in optimal format (ORC or Parquet). At HPCL we ran Hive for the batch ETL pipeline because startup latency didn't matter for overnight jobs. For interactive analyst queries during business hours, Impala or Hive LLAP would have been better choices — we used Power BI with pre-aggregated datasets to work around the latency issue.

GROUP: Hadoop, HDFS & Hive Architecture
Q: What is the difference between Hive and HBase?
A: Hive is a data warehouse layer for batch analytics — it sits on top of HDFS and translates SQL into batch processing jobs. It's optimized for read-heavy analytical workloads with full table scans and aggregations. HBase is a distributed NoSQL database — it stores data in columnar key-value format on HDFS and supports random read/write operations with millisecond latency. HBase is optimized for point lookups and row-level operations. You wouldn't run analytical queries over millions of rows in HBase; you would store operational state and serve individual record lookups. At HPCL Hive was the analytics layer; HBase would have been appropriate for the real-time dashboard serving layer if we'd needed sub-second individual record retrieval. The two are complementary — Hive for analysis, HBase for operational access — and can share the same underlying HDFS.

GROUP: Hadoop, HDFS & Hive Architecture
Q: How does Hive compare to Presto for interactive queries?
A: Presto is an MPP query engine — similar to Impala — with no MapReduce startup overhead and sub-second to few-second latency. Presto can query data from multiple sources simultaneously: HDFS/Hive, Cassandra, MySQL, S3, and others in a single federated query. Hive's strength is deep integration with Hadoop ecosystem features — ACID transactions, fine-grained security via Ranger, Hive Metastore as the catalog, and support for very complex long-running queries. Presto is faster for interactive analytics but less capable for ETL and ACID. At HPCL I would have deployed Presto for the analyst self-service layer if we'd invested in that capability — its federation capability would have let analysts join Hive HDFS data with Oracle procurement data in a single query without ETL. Instead we pre-joined those datasets in the Hive ETL and served aggregated results.

GROUP: Hadoop, HDFS & Hive Architecture
Q: What is the Hadoop ecosystem and what tools complement Hive?
A: The Hadoop ecosystem has grown into a broad set of complementary tools. HDFS provides distributed storage. YARN provides resource management. Hive provides SQL analytics on HDFS. Spark provides fast in-memory distributed processing. HBase provides NoSQL random access. Kafka provides real-time event streaming. Sqoop provides RDBMS-to-HDFS data ingestion. Oozie and Airflow provide workflow orchestration. Ranger and Atlas provide security and governance. Zookeeper provides distributed coordination. Pig provided script-based ETL but is largely deprecated. At HPCL our stack was HDFS, YARN, Hive, Spark, and Sqoop — covering storage, resource management, batch analytics, distributed processing, and RDBMS ingestion. We didn't use Kafka because our data arrival was batch rather than real-time, and we didn't need HBase because our access patterns were analytical rather than operational.

GROUP: Hadoop, HDFS & Hive Architecture
Q: A Hive query that used to take 10 minutes now takes 2 hours — how do you diagnose it?
A: My diagnostic sequence. First: check YARN logs for the specific job — has the resource allocation changed? Is the job getting fewer containers than before? Second: check if data volume grew significantly — a table that was 100 GB six months ago might be 500 GB now, naturally slowing queries proportionally. Third: check table statistics — run ANALYZE TABLE and re-run EXPLAIN to see if the optimizer's plan changed. Stale stats often cause plan regressions when data volume grows. Fourth: check if the file format or partitioning changed — someone may have accidentally created a new partition in text format. Fifth: check for skew — YARN UI task duration distribution for the slowest stage. At HPCL this exact scenario occurred when the fills table grew beyond what was anticipated and statistics became stale — updating stats and adjusting reducer count fixed it.

GROUP: Hadoop, HDFS & Hive Architecture
Q: How do you handle out-of-memory errors in Hive jobs?
A: OOM in Hive typically occurs in one of three places. In the mapper — increase mapreduce.map.memory.mb and mapreduce.map.java.opts (heap size, typically 80% of container memory). In the reducer — increase mapreduce.reduce.memory.mb and the corresponding java.opts. In the Hive client for queries that fetch large result sets — increase hive.client.session.max.rows.fetched or configure result streaming. The underlying cause matters: if mappers are OOM, the task is processing too much data per mapper — reduce the split size to give more tasks with less data each. If reducers are OOM during GROUP BY, increase the reducer count so each handles less data. At HPCL I also enabled hive.exec.compress.intermediate=true to reduce intermediate shuffle data size, which indirectly prevented some reducer OOM conditions by reducing memory pressure from the shuffle buffer.

GROUP: Hadoop, HDFS & Hive Architecture
Q: What are the most common Hive performance anti-patterns?
A: In my experience the top five are: using text format instead of ORC — a 5x performance difference on most queries; not partitioning on commonly filtered columns — forcing full table scans when partition pruning would eliminate 90% of data; chaining too many INSERT INTO operations — each creates new files, accumulating into a small files problem that compounds over time; using SELECT * in CTEs that feed complex downstream queries — pulling all columns even when only two are needed; and running queries without current table statistics, causing the optimizer to make poor join ordering decisions.
